## IMF Executive Board Completes the Combined Third and Fourth Reviews Under the Extended Credit Facility Arrangement with Togo

## FOR IMMEDIATE RELEASE

- The Executive Board of the International Monetary Fund completed the combined third and fourth reviews under the Extended Credit Facility (ECF) arrangement for Togo allowing for an immediate disbursement equivalent to SDR 80.74 million (about USD 109.5 million).
- Program implementation under the ECF arrangement has been broadly satisfactory. Most quantitative performance criteria for the review period were met, and structural reforms are proceeding steadily, with most structural benchmarks achieved.
- The main near -term policy priority is to carefully balance efforts to contain fiscal pressures stemming from the energy price shock while protecting the most vulnerable. Continued structural reforms to address weaknesses in the state -owned enterprises sector, strengthen the financial sector, and improve governance and the business environment will support competitiveness, investment, and inclusive growth over the medium term.

Washington, DC – June 29, 2026: The Executive Board of the International Monetary Fund (IMF) completed the combined third and fourth reviews under the Extended Credit Facility (ECF) arrangement for Togo . The completion of the review enables the immediate disbursement of the equivalent of SDR 80.74 million (about USD 109.5) bringing total disbursements under the arrangement to SDR 220.2 (about USD 298.63 million). In completing the review, the Executive board approved waivers of nonobservance of the quantitative performance criterion (QPC) on the ceiling on net domestic financing, and the continuous QPCs on the ceiling on new external borrowing and the non-accumulation of external arrears based on the minor and temporary nature of nonobservance, reflecting delays in external disbursements, administrative constraints, and evolving financing conditions.

Togo is implementing an economic reform agenda to address the legacies of the COVID-19 pandemic and the increase in global food and fuel prices, while laying the foundations for strong and more inclusive growth. The program includes policies to strengthen fiscal and debt sustainability, protect the poor and vulnerable, address banking sector vulnerabilities, make growth more inclusive, and improve the governance framework. These policies are supported by the ECF arrangement, which was approved by the Executive Board in March 2024 in an amount equivalent to SDR 293.6 million (about USD 403.4 million).

Togo's economy has remained resilient despite global and domestic headwinds. Real GDP grew by about 6 percent in 2025, led by services, while inflation eased. Growth is expected to soften temporarily in 2026 and inflation to rise, mainly due to spillovers from heightened geopolitical tensions, especially the war in the Middle East. Medium-term growth prospects hinge on further progress on structural reforms. Risks remain elevated from regional insecurity, geopolitical tensions, climate shocks and volatile energy and food prices, with financial and external vulnerabilities persisting.

PR26/230

Program implementation under the ECF has been broadly satisfactory. Most quantitative performance criteria for the review period were met, and progress on structural reforms has been strong. Since the completion of the second review, seven of eight structural reforms have been implemented, including measures to improve public financial management, fiscal transparency, and oversight of state-owned enterprises. Social and pro-poor spending targets were also met, reflecting the authorities' commitment to protecting vulnerable groups.

Following the Executive Board discussion on Togo, Mr. Kenji Okamura, Deputy Managing Director, and Acting Chair, issued the following statement: 1

" Despite a challenging regional and external environment, performance under the ECFsupported program has been broadly satisfactory. The economy has remained resilient in the face of successive shocks, and the outlook remains broadly favorable. However, significant downside risks, including persistent security threats, geopolitical tensions, and commodity price volatility, cloud the outlook. It is therefore essential to sustain fiscal consolidation momentum, decisively address financial sector's vulnerabilities and advance structural and governance reforms to achieve sustained and inclusive growth.

"Sustaining fiscal consolidation to preserve debt sustainability and return to the WAEMU fiscal deficit convergence target remains essential. In the short term, the fiscal response to the fuel price shock—particularly the temporary widening of the 2026 deficit to support vulnerable groups — is appropriate. Ongoing reforms in tax policy and administration, including measures to reduce tax expenditures and broaden the tax base, should be swiftly adopted to increase domestic revenue mobilization. While enhancing spending efficiency and maintaining a prudent debt management strategy are key priorities, continued efforts will be needed to protect social and pro-poor spending while containing fiscal risks arising from energy price shocks, revenue shortfalls, and state-owned enterprises (SOEs).

"Addressing banking sector vulnerabilities requires prompt and decisive action. The launch of the independent asset quality review of the weak financial institution is an important step. Timely analysis of risks and vulnerabilities, followed by a credible strategy that safeguards financial stability, ensures transparency, and contains fiscal costs, will be critical to move forward.

"Structural reforms should remain focused on areas critical to supporting growth and mitigating fiscal risks. Priorities include strengthening governance and financial viability in the electricity sector, particularly for the state-owned utility company, and enhancing fiscal reporting, debt transparency, and SOEs oversight. Governance reforms, including stronger anti-corruption frameworks, timely publication and implementation of the Governance Diagnostic Assessment recommendations, and improved AML/CFT effectiveness. "

1 Under the IMF's Articles of Agreement, publication of documents that pertain to member countries is voluntary and requires the member consent. The staff report will be shortly published on the www.imf.org/Togo page.

## TOGO

THIRD AND FOURTH REVIEWS UNDER THE ARRANGEMENT UNDER THE EXTENDED CREDIT FACILITY, REQUESTS FOR WAIVERS OF NON-OBSERVANCE OF QUANTITATIVE PERFORMANCE CRITERIA AND MODIFICATION OF QUANTITATIVE PERFORMANCE CRITERIA

## EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

Context. Fiscal expansion in response to successive shocks supported growth but raised public debt. The ECF arrangement approved in 2024, with the second review completed in June 2025, supports inclusive growth, debt sustainability, and structural reforms. Economic activity is expected to remain resilient despite a temporary slowdown in 2026 due to spillovers from the Middle East war. The outlook is subject to elevated downside risks from the challenging international environment, security threats, financial vulnerabilities, social tensions, and climate risks, which could hinder reform implementation.

Program performance and risks . Program performance was broadly satisfactory, but with the important caveat that the third review was delayed due to the need to recalibrate a credible strategy for a bank. Most quantitative performance criteria (QPCs) for the third and fourth reviews were met. The ceilings on net domestic financing and continuous QPC on new external debt were missed because of temporary cash-flow pressures and a small late-year loan. The non -accumulation of external payment arrears continuous QPC was missed in January 2026 due to administrative delays, but the arrears have been cleared in March . Staff supports waivers because these deviations were minor and did not materially affect fiscal outcomes. The tax revenue indicative target (IT) was also missed for end-December 2025 , highlighting challenges in revenue mobilization. Structural reforms implementation is strong, with most structural benchmarks (SBs) met. Risks to the program remain elevated, including a challenging external environment notably , spillovers from the war in the Middle East , domestic security challenges, weaknesses in implementation capacity, and intensifying climate risks that could undermine progress toward program objectives.

Policy discussions. Staff and the authorities agreed to a temporary widening of the 2026 budget deficit (from 3.0 to 3.4 percent of GDP) to accommodate the impact of the energy shock, including additional outlays to support vulnerable groups and weaker growth. To limit the impact of higher energy prices on public finances, the authorities committed to a gradual domestic fuel prices adjustment and rationalization of non-priority spending, consistent with medium -term consolidation objectives. The deficit is expected to return to 3 percent of GDP by 2027 in line with regional and program commitments. To mitigate fiscal risks stemming from the electricity sector, the authorities—with the support of the World Bank—will

June 11, 2026

## TOGO

implement reforms to strengthen the financial position and governance of the utility company , CEET (Compagnie Energie Electrique du Togo) starting with finalizing its diagnostic in early 2027. In parallel, the authorities will advance broader reforms to enhance governance and strengthen oversight of state -owned enterprises (SOEs) . Finally, staff and authorities agreed on a credible strategy to guide the path forward for a bank and mitigate associated fiscal and financial risks .

Staff views . Staff support the authorities' requests for: (i) completion of the third and fourth reviews under the ECF; (ii) waivers of non-observance of quantitative performance criteria and (iii) modification of quantitative performance criteria . Staff also support the authorities' request to add six new SBs , extend the timeline for two SBs, modify one SB and remove two SBs to better align program design with the authorities' reform plans and capacity development needs .

Approved By Vitaliy Kramarenko (AFR) and Jacques Miniane (SPR)

Discussions took place in Washington during April 13-16, virtually during April 28-30 and in Lomé during May 11-19, 2026. The staff team comprised Mr. T. Kinda (head), Mr. M. Nshimiyimana, Mr. C . Amo -Yartey , Mr. N. Sawadogo, Ms. R . Mowatt, Ms. M. Chu (all AFR), Mr. J. Fanning (SPR), Ms. R. Boukezia (Resident Representative) and Mr. K.D. Assogbavi (local economist) . Mr. V. Kramarenko (AFR) attended some policy discussion meetings. Ms. E. Boukpessi (ED office) also attended mission meetings. The team met the Minister of Finance and Budget, the interim National Director of BCEAO , other senior officials of the national authorities, the private sector, and development partners. Ms. K. Dereje provided administrative assistance.

4

## CONTEXT

1. Togo's economy has remained resilient to successive shocks in recent years, helped in part by fiscal support, but development challenges and security threats persist . While economic growth has continued to demonstrate resilience despite a challenging global and domestic environment , food insecurity remains high , affecting at least 22.6 percent of the population . About one-third of the population lives below the extreme poverty line (US$3.00 a day) and significant gaps remain with respect to several Sustainable Development Goals. In addition, the security situation remains fragile due to persistent terrorist threats in the north .

## 2. The fiscal expansion implemented in response to earlier shocks helped mitigate their impact but also led to a spike in public debt, making substantial fiscal adjustment necessary .

Measures aimed at preserving households' purchasing power, including large untargeted fuel subsidies , pushed the fiscal deficit to 8.3 percent of GDP in 2022 and public debt peaked at 66.2 percent of GDP in 2024 . The deficit was reduced to 6.7 percent of GDP in 2023, largely reflecting a gradual phaseout of fuel subsidies. The ECF arrangement initiated in 2024 supports the authorities' efforts to strengthen debt sustainability while advancing structural reforms to promote inclusive growth and mitigate fiscal and financial sector risks.

## RECENT ECONOMIC DEVELOPMENTS

3. Growth remained resilient in 2025 , while inflation eased . After robust growth of 6.3 percent in 2024, activity remained strong in 2025 despite a temporary slowdown in the second quarter of 2025 due to social protests . However, stronger port and airport traffic, supported in part by improved capacity and throughput , sustained momentum and helped keep growth at around 6½ percent in 2025 . 1 End of period headline inflation fell to 0.7 percent in April 2026, driven mainly by lower food prices , although the effects of higher international energy prices have not yet fully materialized .
4. Tax revenue underperformed in 2025 , but the planned fiscal consolidation remained on track (Text Table 1) . Tax revenue rose by 3.9 percent y-o-y , but reached only 93 percent of target, falling by 0.4 percent of GDP relative to 2024, mainly owing to higher tax expenditures and weak implementation of planned measures. Nevertheless, the authorities reduced the fiscal deficit (commitment basis) to 3.2 percent of GDP in 2025, from 3.9 percent in the revised budget and 6.8 percent in 2024, through expenditure compression, notably in goods and services and domestically financed investment. End -year financing constraints, however, led to floats of about 0.6 percent of GDP.

1 The macro framework incorporates the authorities' latest final national accounts for 2020–23, implying a sizeable upward revision to nominal GDP levels and the subsequent nominal GDP path. High-frequency indicators and full year output estimates also point to a material upward revision for 2024 and 2025 growth , with growth estimated at 6 . 3 percent in both years . GDP -based economic ratios are mechanically lower, all else equal.

Fiscal consolidation helped lower public debt and preserve a moderate risk of overall

and external debt distress . The smaller primary deficit—and, to a lesser extent, upwards GDP revision — reduced the public debt-to-GDP ratio to 64.9 percent at end-2025. Revenue and grants shortfalls increased short -term liquidity risks, but these were mitigated by the refinancing of a shortterm external loan with World Bank support . Public debt is assessed as sustainable. The PV of public debt is projected to fall below the program anchor of 55 percent of GDP in 2026 even after accommodating potential outlays related to energy-sector deficits , and to remain below it under a scenario where further banking sector operations would be required in 2027 1 .

6. Exposure to the regional market declined and financing conditions eased . The mobilization of concessional budget support, non-concessional external loans guaranteed by MDBs, and commercial loans reduced regional financing needs . Easing financing conditions allowed Togo to shift issuance on the regional market from T-bills to bonds , reducing rollover risks , w while T -bill yields declined .
7. Although most banks comply with prudential norms, vulnerabilities persist in parts of the banking system . Nonperforming loans increased markedly to about 13 percent of total credit at

1 Togo's debt carrying capacity was increased from medium to strong in the second review, which meant the threshold for a high risk of overall public debt distress was increased from 55 to 70 percent of GDP. At the same time, the Executive Board also agreed to move the target date for achieving the debt target from 2026 to 2027.

end -December, from 8.1 percent in September 2025, alongside a sharp rise in restructured loans , primarily due to a deterioration of asset quality in a few banks, including one public bank . However, the authorities are considering measures—including the development of a credible strategy for one weak bank (see ¶¶23–24) and the securitization of loans into marketable bonds—to contain the rise in nonperforming loans (NPLs) . The provision coverage ratio also declined during the same period .

8. Togo's external position weakened slightly in 2025 but remained broadly in line with fundamentals and desirable policies . Higher port traffic, much of it handled by non-resident freight service providers, reduced net service exports and offset a smaller goods trade deficit driven mainly by lower imports. Export performance was subdued owing to lower prices for key exports. The current account deficit widened to 2 . 7 percent of GDP , while regional reserves remained well above adequacy thresholds, at about 8 . 4 months of imports by April 2026, supported by improved terms of trade (Annex I) .

<!-- image -->

## OUTLOOK AND RISKS

9. Macroeconomic performance is projected to moderate further in the near term (Text Table 2) . Growth is expected to slow but remain solid at 5 percent in 2026, reflecting spillovers from the war in the Middle East, before recovering to 5.3 percent in 2027 and 5.5 percent thereafter as the drag from the conflict dissipates . Over the medium term, higher public investments, supported by improved domestic revenue mobilization , and productivity-enhancing structural reforms are expected to support growth. Headline inflation is expected to spike to 2.8 percent in 2026 , driven by higher energy and food prices, before easing toward the central bank for west African states ' (BCEAO) 2-percent target . 2 The current account deficit is projected to widen to about 4.2 percent of GDP in 2026 and to remain above previous projections , reflecting weaker terms of trade, notably from higher near-term energy prices .

## 10. The outlook is subject to elevated downside risks (Annex II) . Externally , rising geopolitical

tensions and an escalation of conflicts — together with disruptions to trade routes and supply chains—could weaken global demand and weigh on Togo's exports. Higher and more volatile energy and fertilizer prices could raise food prices, worsen food insecurity, and heighten risks of social unrest . An illustrative downside scenario (Annex III and Text Figure 2) highlights the negative impact of higher oil prices, including on growth. A higher fuel import bill and lower remittance inflows could further strain the external position. Domestically, terrorism and security threats in the north remain significant risks. Renewed social discontent could complicate revenue mobilization and tariff adjustments

<!-- image -->

needed for cost recovery . Persistent vulnerabilities in the financial sector also remain. Climate -related shocks pose growing risks to activity , while governance weaknesses could deter investment and limit access to commercial external financing .

2 Baseline fuel price assumptions are based on weekly futures prices as of March 27, 2026, with Average Petroleum Spot Price (APSP) oil prices projected at about US$91 per barrel in 2026 and US$79 per barrel in 2027.

## PROGRAM PERFORMANCE

11. Performance under the ECF -supported program has been broadly satisfactory , but with the important caveat that the third review was delayed due to the need to recalibrate a credible strategy for a bank:

- Four of the five QPCs were met for each review . For the third review, the QPC on the ceiling on net domestic financing for end-June 2025 was missed (by 0.3 percent of GDP) due to higher-than-anticipated short-term borrowing from the banking sector to smooth cash flows, partly due to a delay in the ECF disbursement . 3 For the fourth review, the continuous QPC on the ceiling on the contracting of new external debt was missed in December 2025 by 0.4 percent of GDP because of a loan contracted at end -year (but not disbursed) . The tax revenue IT for end -December 2025 was missed by 6.5 percent , equivalent to 0.9 percent of GDP.
- Two continuous QPCs were missed in 2026 . External payment arrears to a Paris Club creditor amounting to CFAF 1.3 billion (0.02 percent of GDP) were accumulated in January 2026 , because of administrative delays, and were cleared in March 2026 . The continuous QPC on the contracting of new external debt was missed by 0.8 percent of GDP in March 2026 , reflecting changed circumstances and greater space under the debt sustainability analysis (DSA)thresholds that allowed higher borrowing without compromising debt sustainability or program objectives .
- Implementation of structural reforms has been strong, with seven out of eight SBs completed since the second review . Completed measures include: (i) incorporating stateowned enterprises (SOEs) and publicly guaranteed debt into the debt bulletin; (ii) identifying VAT exemptions; (iii) preparing a report on civil service taxation; (iv) issuing orders mandating the regular production of public enterprises' financial statements; (v) publishing beneficial ownership information for firms awarded procurement contracts; (vi) reducing operations under customs suspension procedures; and (vii) adopting a ministerial decree eliminating at least 0.25 percent of GDP in VAT exemptions. The remaining SB—on the drivers of unexplained debt—was implemented with delay in May , as additional time was needed to finalize the report.
- However, the end-December 2024 SB (SB 7.1) on raising capital to the regulatory minimum for a bank, previously assessed as met, was not met. After reassessment of the

<!-- image -->

3 The ECF disbursement took place in July instead of June , as budgeted.

bank's financial position, it has become clear that the authorities' capital injection, while consistent with the agreed amount, was insufficient to reach the regulatory minimum.

## POLICY DISCUSSIONS

12. Policy discussions focused on the implementation of the ECF-supported program , with emphasis on (i) strengthening debt sustainability while enhancing inclusion , consistent with the recalibrated fiscal anchor; and (ii) advancing structural reforms to support growth and mitigate fiscal and financial sector risks .

## A. Strengthening Debt Sustainability While Enhancing Inclusion

Near -term Fiscal Policy—Navigating the Energy Shock

13. The 2026 energy price shock is expected to place significant pressure on public finances and generate fiscal costs of about 1 percent of GDP. In response, the authorities have begun adjusting fuel prices 4 and plan further gradual increases depending on international price developments. Given constraints in the targeting capacity of existing social protection schemes, including incomplete coverage, the authorities intend to limit their use while accelerating the development of a comprehensive and targeted social safety net system . In the interim, the authorities plan to use price differentiation across fuel products to complement support to vulnerable groups. If the shock intensifies, the authorities intend to take additional measures, including further fuel price adjustments and targeted support, to safeguard program objectives and protect vulnerable households (MEFP ¶14).

## 14. Staff and the authorities agreed on a limited and temporary relaxation of the 2026 fiscal deficit — by about 0.4 percent of GDP—to accommodate support to vulnerable groups .

The authorities adopted an advance decree in early June 2026, to be followed by a supplementary budget in due course, targeting a deficit of 3.4 percent of GDP (compared with the initial target of 3 percent). The increase in the fiscal deficit mainly reflects support for vulnerable groups, including through differentiated fuel price adjustment and a modest contribution from automatic stabilizers (0.1 percent of GDP) owing to lower growth . 5 Despite the energy price shock, fiscal policy in 2026 remains anchored on revenue mobilization and expenditure rationalization measures.

4 On May 26, the authorities increased pump prices for gasoline by 7 percent (from CFAF 680 to CFAF 725 per liter), diesel by 8 percent (from CFAF 695 to CFAF 750 per liter), and kerosene by 60 percent (from CFAF 650 to CFAF 1,040 per liter).

5 The fiscal cost of the energy price shock (1 percent of GDP without any measures) is expected to be covered by (i) envisaged price increases (0.3 percent of GDP); (ii) postponement of under-executed non-priority capital spending (0.5 percent of GDP), and (iii) an accommodation of support to vulnerable groups through differentiated fuel price adjustment (0.2 percent of GDP) . Other limited targeted support includes the distribution of solar lighting kits to marginalized groups heavily impacted by fuel price increases . Differentiated fuel price adjustments account for fuel products mostly used by vulnerable groups (for instance drivers of taxi motorbikes or " zemidjan " ) and limiting relatively price increases associated with of these products .

## 15. On the revenue side, the authorities aim to increase revenue by at least 0.5 percent of GDP, mainly through tax policy measures and stronger tax administration (Text Table 3).

- Tax policy measures (0.4 percent of GDP) include introducing new taxes on: (i) selected agricultural exports (soybeans, cashew, and shea nuts), (ii) gambling winnings above CFAF 500,000, and (iii) digital services. Moreover, in March 2026, the authorities issued a decree eliminating some VAT exemptions— including those introduced during the pandemic— with an estimated yield of at least 0.25 percent of GDP (end-March 2026 SB, met), and plan to expand property taxation . A supplementary budget would be warranted should expected yields from revenue measures in the 2026 budget fall short of expectations .

·

Revenue administration measures (0.1 percent of GDP) include halving expired and

undischarged customs suspension procedures (end-March 2026 SB 8.2, met), strengthening audit activity, and stepping up tax arrears recovery.

16. On the expenditure side, the authorities are balancing energy-shock mitigation, social protection, and priority spending. Total spending is projected to rise by about 0.3 percent of GDP from the original budget to 20.8 percent of GDP, reflecting higher energy costs and targeted transfers (about 0.8 percent of GDP), partly offset by savings of about 0.5 percent of GDP from underexecuted public investment. Social spending will be preserved, including through continued implementation of the emergency program in the Savanes region .

## 17. A diversified financing mix is

expected to cover gross financing needs in 2026. Gross financing needs are estimated at 15.2 percent of GDP, reflecting debt amortization (11.8 percent of GDP) and the fiscal deficit (3.4 percent of GDP). These needs are expected to be met through budget support (2.2 percent of GDP), project loans (3 percent of GDP), external commercial borrowing (3.5 percent of GDP), and regional market financing (6.5 percent of GDP) (Text Table 4).

## Medium Term Fiscal Policy

18. Despite the short-term impact of the energy price shock, the authorities remain committed to medium -term fiscal consolidation to safeguard debt sustainability while protecting priority spending . Reducing the fiscal deficit to 3 percent of GDP by 2027 and maintaining a moderate risk of debt distress remain the key medium-term fiscal objectives.
19. Future revenue reforms will be guided by the forthcoming Medium Term Revenue Mobilization Strategy (MTRS) . Preparatory work is advancing with IMF technical assistance, and the deadline to finalize the strategy (SB 8.3, initially due by end-June 2026) has been extended to end -September 2026 to ensure reform quality and coherence. Following adoption, the authorities will identify measures to raise revenue by at least 0.5 percent of GDP annually (proposed SB 8.4, end -December 2026), increasing the tax-to-GDP ratio from 13.7 percent in 2026 to 15.7 percent by 2030. However, the medium-term revenue strategy is ambitious—aiming for annual tax revenue gains of at least ½ percent of GDP—and the challenges to achieving these targets should not be underestimated .

## 20. The medium -term fiscal framework aims to balance consolidation with the

preservation and scaling up of priority spending. Current spending is projected to decline gradually, reflecting lower interest costs from increased concessional financing and the phased elimination of fuel subsidies, while public investment is set to rise in line with improved revenue mobilization. Social and pro-poor spending , together with strengthened targeting, will be maintained and expanded, including through the emergency program in the north. The authorities will also pursue efforts to enhance the efficiency of public spending, notably through the digitalization of public procurement.

## 21. The authorities will maintain prudent debt management and prioritize concessional external financing while rebalancing the portfolio toward longer-term, lower-cost instruments

(MEFP ¶23). Consistent with the 2024–26 medium-term debt strategy (MTDS), this shift has been supported by external financing mobilized in early 2026 and reduced reliance on the regional market. The authorities intend to update the debt strategy to reflect evolving market conditions. In the interim, they will avoid new short-term external borrowing, carefully assess the costs and risks of non-concessional financing, and explore refinancing options for maturing short-term obligations.

## B. Structural Reforms to Support Growth and Limit Risks

## Strengthening the Banking System

## 22. The authorities aim to strengthen prudent credit provision by improving credit information, enhancing regulatory compliance, and reinforcing banking sector resilience .

Together with strict adherence to prudential standards, these efforts aim to bolster resilience and sustain public confidence in the banking system (MEFP ¶27) .

23. A financial institution poses risks to financial stability . A supervisory assessment conducted in 2025 found that the institution's financial position was significantly weaker than previously reported, reflecting elevated nonperforming loans , large capital needs, and governance shortcomings . In response, the authorities reported to have taken several corrective measures , including appointing an experienced senior manager, establishing a new supervisory board of recognized private banking experts, and adopting updated risk management guidelines , including credit risk. The authorities have also reported steps taken to improve the institution's balance sheet, including through enhanced loan recovery focused on large debtors , transfers of selected non-performing loans, and loan reclassifications.
24. To establish the institution's current financial position and inform a credible strategy , the authorities commissioned an independent assessment . The asset quality review (AQR , prior action) , launched in May 2026 and to be conducted by a reputable international firm under terms of reference agreed with IMF staff, is expected to be completed by end-October 2026 (proposed SB 12.1, end-October 2026) . The authorities intend to use the AQR findings to choose a credible strategy while safeguarding financial stability and containing fiscal costs (proposed SB 12.2 and SB 12.3).

## Electricity Sector Reform

25. Electricity sector losses remain a significant fiscal risk . Two SOEs operate in Togo's electricity sector, with CEET responsible for distribution. CEET continues to incur sizable losses because tariffs remain below cost -recovery levels and technical and commercial losses are high . Recent estimates suggest that CEET's losses reached CFAF 25 billion (0.4 percent of GDP) in 2025, while its debt stood at about US$550 million (5.2 percent of GDP) at end-2024, of which nearly 52 percent was on -lent by the government. The gap between tariffs and cost recovery was estimated at 22 percent in 2024. To address CEET's weak financial position, the authorities implemented an average tariff increase of 12.5 percent in 2025, while protecting vulnerable households.
26. To restore CEET's financial viability and contain fiscal risks, the authorities will implement a sequenced reform agenda. In the near term, they will clear arrears to independent power producers and establish a reform coordination mechanism. With World Bank support, they will complete a comprehensive institutional, financial, technical, and operational diagnostic of CEET (proposed end-March 2027 SB). In parallel, they will implement measures to reduce costs and improve operational and commercial performance. Based on the diagnostic, the authorities will adopt a time-bound performance contract with CEET management that sets explicit quantitative targets, defines priority reform actions and milestones, clarifies responsibilities, and establishes a robust monitoring framework to ensure implementation of the identified reforms. The performance contract and diagnostic will guide the definition of future SBs to support CEET reforms. Over the medium term, these reforms could focus on introducing a cost-reflective multi-year tariff framework with automatic adjustment mechanisms, strengthening sector governance, and improving planning (MEFP ¶33).

## Strengthening Public Financial Management, Including the Oversight and Management of SOEs

## 27. The authorities are intensifying efforts to identify and address the factors behind

faster -than -expected public debt accumulation (Annex V) . Over 2017 – 24, debt increased by 0.9 percent of GDP annually beyond what standard debt dynamics would predict. Following earlier reconciliation work, a comprehensive diagnostic report was finalized in May 2026, after delays in developing a mitigation strategy (missed end-December 2025 SB 9.1). Corrective measures are being implemented, including monthly publication in the Government Financial Operations Table (TOFE) of commercial banks' net claims on the central government and regular reconciliation exercises. The authorities committed to including, for 2025, stock-flow adjustment analysis in the public debt bulletin6 and publishing a report on government financial operations, including reconciliation between the Treasury net position and the net government position reported by the BCEAO (modified SB 9.2 proposed, end-June 2026). Systematic reporting of above- and below-the-line reconciliation in budget execution reports will also continue. In line with international best practices, the authorities also plan to transition to GFSM 2014, supported by IMF technical assistance.

## 28. The authorities will strengthen oversight and management of SOEs and further

enhance the public-private partnership (PPP) framework to mitigate fiscal risks, reinforce fiscal discipline, and improve service delivery. Significant progress has been made in strengthening SOE governance, including adoption of a new legal framework, expanded debt reporting coverage to include SOE debt and government guarantees (SB 5.3), and requirements for audited financial statements for all SOEs (end-December 2025 SB 5.1, met). Efforts are also underway to professionalize management and enhance performance monitoring. For this, the authorities will need to accelerate the adoption of implementation texts of the SOEs law issued in April 2025 . On PPPs, a dedicated coordination unit is being operationalized following the adoption of the legal framework, and the project pipeline will be updated under the forthcoming strategy. The authorities will continue strengthening institutional and oversight frameworks, including by integrating PPPrelated fiscal risk assessments into the budget process (MEFP ¶31).

6 Stock -flow analysis for 2025 was included in the end-December 2025 public debt bulletin , published on April 10, 2026.

## Enhancing Governance, Inclusion, and the Business Environment

29. The authorities are strengthening social protection systems to enhance inclusion and resilience. They are gradually scaling up social policies, including with World Bank support, through a flagship social safety nets and economic inclusion program targeting vulnerable populations with cash -for -work schemes, economic empowerment measures, and unconditional cash transfers. To enhance impact, they plan a gradual and targeted increase in social spending, consistent with available fiscal space and debt sustainability. Efforts to modernize the social protection system will continue, including strengthening targeting and transparency through the rollout of a biometricbased social registry, with a pilot phase expected by end-December 2026 (proposed structural benchmark). Enhanced monitoring, evaluation, and reporting frameworks will support effective targeting and efficient execution of social spending (MEFP ¶28–29).
30. The authorities are advancing reforms to improve the business climate, including through digitalization, with World Bank support. Key measures include automating environmental permitting, rolling out a digital platform for commercial dispute resolution, and enhancing public procurement and port information systems to strengthen efficiency and trade facilitation. Sustaining reforms would require addressing persistent constraints—such as public payment delays, costly and unreliable electricity, and infrastructure gaps—to support formalization and promote inclusive, private sector–led growth (MEFP ¶36–37).
31. The authorities have taken initial steps to address significant governance gaps, but further effort is needed . They amended the public procurement framework to require publication of beneficial ownership information for companies awarded public contracts (end-December 2025 SB 6.1, met). They requested an IMF Governance Diagnostic Assessment (GDA), which was conducted in February 2026, and intend to publish the report (end-February 2027, SB 10.1) 7 . In the interim, the authorities have been taking steps to enhance transparency, including mandatory disclosure of public procurement awardees and enhanced publication of revenue administration information. They also plan to update the legal framework to enable digitalization of commercial dispute resolution. These reforms should strengthen transparency and acceptability of reforms (MEFP ¶34).
32. The authorities recognize that AML/CFT risks remain elevated, particularly those related to tax evasion, corruption, and terrorism financing. In response, a new AML/CFT law aligned with the 2023 WAEMU Directive was adopted in February 2026, strengthening compliance with FATF standards and broadening the regulatory perimeter. While this represents an important step, implementation challenges persist. The authorities are therefore committed to strengthening institutional capacities, operationalizing the beneficial ownership registry, and extending supervision to high-risk non-financial sectors (MEFP ¶35).

7 The need for multi -stakeholder consultation before such a publication would require time, warranting the adjustment of the SB timeline

## PROGRAM MODALITIES AND OTHER ISSUES

33. Staff supports the authorities' request for waivers for the nonobservance of the QPC on the ceiling on net domestic financing and the continuous QPCs on the ceiling on new external borrowing and the non-accumulation of external arrears. The breaches were minor and temporary, reflecting delays in external disbursements, administrative factors, and evolving financing conditions. In 2025, the breach of the net domestic financing QPC at end-June was temporary and corrected by end-December, while the nonobservance of the continuous QPC on new external borrowing resulted from the signing of a loan that caused a limited breach (about 0.4 percent of GDP), without immediate budgetary impact because it was not disbursed. In 2026, the breach of the continuous QPC on external arrears was minor (0.02 percent of GDP), arose from administrative delays , and was promptly cleared. The ceiling on new external borrowing was exceeded by about 0.8 percent of GDP at end-March , which staff deem to be minor because the new loans signed do not undermine program objectives amid increased space under DSA thresholds. Given the temporary nature of most deviations, the changed circumstances, the corrective actions taken, and the authorities' continued commitment to the program, staff consider that program objectives remain achievable and support the requested waivers.

## 34. Conditionality comprises semi-annual quantitative performance criteria, continuous performance criteria, quarterly indicative targets, and structural benchmarks through endJune 2027 (MEFP Table 1 and 2)

- The authorities request, and staff support, modifications for the end-June 2026 QPCs on the floor on the basic primary balance , the ceiling on net domestic financing , and the ceiling on the contracting of new external debt (and its adjustor) . These three QPCs are proposed for endDecember 2026 and end -June 2027 .
- Staff support the authorities' request to modify , extend timelines and drop the following SBs:
- o Drop SB 7.2 on adoption of the restructuring plan for the remaining public bank" (endMarch 2026) and SB 7.3 on implementation of the restructuring plan for the remaining public bank (end-December 2026). These SBs were replaced by two other banking sector SBs (see the last bullet point below)
- o Extend the timeline of SBs 8.3 on adoption of the MTRS and 10.1 on publication of the GDA to allow sufficient time to ensure the quality and coherence of reforms .
- o Modify SB 9.2 on the publication of (i) the stock-flow adjustments in the public debt statistics bulletin; and (ii) the report on government financial operations, including an explanation of the discrepancy between the net Treasury position (TOFE) and the net government position as reported by the BCEAO .
- o Six new SBs are proposed, covering revenue mobilization (one), the banking sector (three), social inclusion (one), and governance (one).

35. Fund financial support under the ECF-arrangement will be used for budgetary purposes in accordance with rules for the use of Fund resources for budget support (EBS/10/55).
36. The program is fully financed, with firm commitments for the 12 months following completion of this review. The 2026 financing gap is projected at US$301 million (Table 6) compared with US$203 million at the second review, reflecting additional financing needs arising from the impact of the war in the Middle East. It is expected to be covered by ECF disbursements (SDR 110 million, about US$151 million) and higher World Bank budget support (US$150 million) , which offset the expiration of prior EU and AfDB budget support . The financing gap is projected to narrow to US$145 million in 2027 , with a larger share financed by the World Bank and a commensurate reduction in the IMF's burden share . There are good prospects for adequate financing for the remainder of the program.

## 37. Togo's capacity to repay the IMF remains adequate but subject to significant risks.

Credit outstanding is projected to peak at 253.8 percent of quota in 2026 and remain broadly around the middle of the historical distribution of past PRGT arrangements when measured against GDP and external debt . Debt service to the IMF exceeds the 75 th percentile of PRGT borrowers over the next year when measured against revenue and exports. Ongoing fiscal consolidation has improved debt solvency indicators , and further revenue mobilization will support capacity to repay through improved debt dynamics while maintaining Togo's moderate risk of overall and external debt distress. World Bank support to mitigate elevated external debt service in 2026 has eased liquidity pressures. Risks from a deterioration in the security situation and from SOE-related contingent liabilities are mitigated by policy measures. Togo also has a strong track record of repaying the IMF.

38. Safeguards assessment . The most recent assessment, completed in August 2023, found that the BCEAO continued to have well established audit arrangements and a strong control environment. Implementation of a priority recommendation, to align the BCEAO's Statute with the Cooperation Agreement of December 21, 2019, signed between the WAEMU member States and the French Republic, remains pending, as it requires ratification of the said agreement by all WAEMU member countries. To date, four out of the eight member States have ratified the Agreement. Nevertheless, several of its provisions are already being applied. Staff continue to engage with the authorities on follow -up efforts.
39. A new Poverty Reduction and Growth Strategy is under preparation. Work on the 202630 National Development Plan began in October 2025 with consultations taking place in May 2026. The authorities request that this new strategy be presented at the fifth review. In the meantime, the 2020 -25 roadmap will continue to serve as the main policy anchor.

## 40. Capacity development priorities are aligned with program objectives (Annex IV) .

## STAFF APPRAISAL

41. Togo's economy has remained resilient to successive shocks in recent years , and the outlook is broadly favorable but subject to downside risks . Growth is projected to slow but remain robust at 5.0 percent in 2026, reflecting the impact of the Middle East war, before recovering gradually in the medium term. Inflation is expected to rise temporarily and the current account deficit to widen in the near -term. Downside risks dominate the outlook, including security threats, financial sector weaknesses, geopolitical tensions, commodity price volatility, potential social unrest , and climate risks.
42. Program performance was broadly satisfactory , but with the important caveat that the third review was delayed due to the need to recalibrate a credible strategy for a bank . Most QPCs for the third and fourth reviews were met . The ceilings on net domestic financing and new external debt were missed because of temporary cash-flow pressures and a small late-year loan. Staff supports waivers because these deviations were minor and did not materially affect fiscal outcomes. The tax revenue IT was also missed, highlighting challenges in revenue mobilization. Structural reform implementation remained strong, with most benchmarks met. The bank recapitalization benchmark, previously assessed as met , is now considered not met after reassessment of the bank's financial position.
43. The fiscal response to the fuel price shock is appropriate, but sustaining fiscal consolidation momentum will be essential . The temporary widening of the deficit in 2026 to provide support to vulnerable groups is adequate. Offsetting measures through stronger revenue mobilization and expenditure rationalization will be essential to sustain progress toward the WAEMU 3 percent of GDP fiscal deficit convergence target. Protecting social and pro-poor spending remains appropriate , and implementation will need to contain risks , including energy price shocks and revenue shortfalls.
44. Structural reforms should focus on priorities most critical to growth and fiscal risk reduction. Payment delays, costly and unreliable electricity, and infrastructure gaps continue to weigh on private sector-led growth. Restoring the financial viability of the electricity sector through cost reduction, stronger governance at CEET, and, if needed, gradual tariff adjustment will be essential to reduce quasi-fiscal losses. Continued progress in fiscal and debt reporting and SOE oversight should strengthen transparency and risk management. Governance reforms, including stronger anti-corruption frameworks, the GDA agenda, and improved AML/CFT effectiveness and supervision, remain important to boost policy credibility.
45. Banking sector vulnerabilities require prompt action. The independent external review of the weak financial institution should be completed without delay and followed by a credible strategy, with safeguards to ensure transparency and contain fiscal costs. In the interim, staff emphasizes the need for continued prudence in the financial institution's daily operations to prevent further risk accumulation in the period ahead . Stronger supervision, governance, and transparency across the banking sector will be critical to restoring confidence and safeguarding financial stability.
46. In light of the authorities' commitment and broadly satisfactory program performance, together with the recalibrated strategy for the bank, staff supports (i) completion of the Third and Fourth Reviews under the ECF arrangement; (ii) waivers for non-observance of performance criteria on net domestic financing, new external debt, and non-accumulation of external arrears; (iii) modification of quantitative performance criteria on the basic primary fiscal balance, net domestic financing, and new external debt for end-June 2026; (iv) and disbursement of the fourth and fifth tranches under the arrangement totaling SDR 80.74 million

Access to electricity is somewhat higher in Togo than in comparators, with more than half of the population connected, but rural areas lag far behind.

1 As reported by Country authorities according to their classification of creditors except Eximbank China and Eximbank India are reported by the authorities as commercial creditors, but classified as bilateral non -Paris Club here and in the DSA. Debt coverage is the same as the DSA.

2 Debt is collateralized when the creditor has rights over an asset or revenue stream that would allow it, if the borrower defaults on its payment obligations, to rely on the asset or revenue stream to secure repayment of the debt. Collateralization entails a borrower granting liens over specific existing assets or future receivables to a lender as security against repayment of the loan. Collateral is "unrelated" when it has no relationship to a project financed by the loan. n example would be borrowing to finance the budget deficit, collateralized by oil revenue receipts. See the joint IMF-World Bank note for the G20 "Collateralized Transactions: Key Considerations for Public enders and Borrowers" for a discussion of issues raised by collateral.

3 Includes other -one off guarantees not included in publicly guaranteed debt (e.g. credit lines) and other explicit contingent liabilities not elsewhere classified (e.g. potential legal claims, payments resulting from PPP arrangements).

1 Cumulated from the beginning of the calendar year.

2 The basic primary fiscal balance is defined as the difference between (i) the government's total fiscal revenue on a cash basis (tax and nontax) excluding grants and (ii) basic primary fiscal expenditure, excluding the banking sector repair transactions of up to CFAF 27 billion. The balance will be calculated cumulatively from the beginning of the calendar year. The floor will be adjusted downwards by the amount of additional external budget support (grants and loans) that exceeds the projected external budget support for the program, up to a maximum of CFAF 18 billion.

3 The ceiling will be adjusted downwards (upwards) depending on external budget support (grants and program loans) and other non-project loans ("other loans"), excluding IMF disbursements. (i) The net domestic financing ceiling will be adjusted downward by the amount of the surplus of external budget support and other non-project loans projected under the program, less 18 billion CFA francs. (ii) The net domestic financing ceiling will be adjusted upward by the amount of the deficit between the external budget support and other non-project loans (excluding projected and ongoing IMF program disbursements) projected under the program and that which will be realized, up to a maximum of 50 billion CFA francs.

4 (i) 2024 targets are cumulated from January 1, 2024. Targets for each year thereafter are cumulated from January 1 of the year. (ii) An adjustor of up to 5 percent of the external debt ceiling set in PV terms applies to this ceiling, in case deviations from the performance criterion on the PV of new external debt are prompted by a change in the financing terms (interest, maturity, grace period, payment schedule, upfront commissions, management fees) of a debt or debts. The adjustor cannot be applied when deviations are prompted by an increase in the nominal amount of total debt contracted or guaranteed.

5 Including debt owed to BOAD and excluding IMF resources. For end-December 2025, the ceiling was adjusted downwards by CFAF 34 billion because not all World Bank loans in the borrowing plan were signed in 2025. For 2026, the ceiling will be adjusted upwards to accommodate concessional budget support and World Bank loans, along with an adjustor for a change in financing terms, as specified in the TMU. The adjustors for 2027 are set on an indicative basis.

6 Social and pro-poor spending as defined includes expenditure executed from the Government budget (from both domestic and external budget support), relating mainly to public interventions in the areas of education, health and nutrition, the establishment of social safety nets, access to electricity, water and sanitation, microfinance (small and medium enterprises), as well as law and justice.

Note: Precise definitions and details are included in the Technical Memorandum of Understanding (TMU).

## Annex I . External Sector Assessment

Overall Assessment: Togo's external position is estimated to have been broadly in line with the level implied by mediumterm fundamentals and desirable policies in 2025, unchanged from 2024. The current account deficit is estimated to have widened slightly in 2025, 5, reflecting weaker export and remittance growth. The deficit is projected to widen in the short term on account of higher fuel import prices and a worsening term of trade outlook following the war in the Middle East. It is projected to narrow in the medium term, as fiscal restraint and increased domestic production helps reduce imports, s, while exports are bolstered by improved trade infrastructure and services .

Potential Policy Responses: While no major external adjustment is warranted given the small estimated gaps, allowing greater pass -through of higher fuel prices would help contain domestic demand pressure and mitigate the impact of external shocks . Structural reforms to improve competitiveness, including strengthening the business environment, t, improving infrastructure and enhancing trade facilitation , would help attract more FDI and diversify exports and boost resilience . Strengthening the financial sector would support further investment and competitiveness while containing debt and financial risks. Over the longer term, investment in human capital and well-targeted social safety nets would support inclusive growth .

## Foreign Assets and Liabilities: Position and Trajectory

Background. Togo's net international investment position (NIIP) in 2023 declined slightly to -6 percent of GDP (based on the most recent data) from -5 percent in 2022. Gross assets increased steadily in nominal terms since 2018 but dropped to 109 percent of GDP in 2023 from a peak of 117 percent of GDP in 2021. The composition of assets has evolved, notably to a higher share of portfolio investment (29 percent of GDP) and other investment (27 percent of GDP) and a lower share of direct investment (34 percent of GDP). Gross liabilities as a share of GDP declined in 2023, driven by a fall in outward direct investment to 24 percent of GDP following elevated levels of more than 30 percent of GDP over 2019-21. Public borrowing from securities (24 percent of GDP) and loans (24 percent of GDP) make up less than half of gross liabilities. Historical data show that more than 60 percent of liabilities are in other WAEMU countries, indicating regional concentration in exposure. More than 40 percent of assets are in the WAEMU region .

Assessment. The current NIIP and its projected path do not imply risks to external sustainability and substantial current account adjustment is not needed. The high levels of assets and liabilities reflect transactions by regional banks based in Togo and emanate from relatively stable flows; improvements to the business environment would help increase inward FDI. Debt liabilities fell slightly, and the DSA assesses that Togo has a moderate risk of external debt distress based on more recent data. Further, around a fifth of debt liabilities are to the BCEAO, which is deemed lower risk than other types of flows. The authorities' rebalancing of borrowing from regional CFAF securities to external concessional loans is estimated to have reduced portfolio liabilities in 2025 and increased other investment liabilities, thereby reducing risks.

## Current Account

Background. Togo has run persistent moderate current account deficits in recent years. Trade goods deficits averaging around 12 percent of GDP over 2020-24 have been offset by an average trade service surplus of 2 percent of GDP, driven mainly by services linked to air and port traffic, and a secondary income balance of 7 percent of GDP, notably from remittances. The deficit widened in 2022 as imports surged amidst high food and fuel import prices and reached 3.8 percent of GDP in 2023 as remittances moderated . Fiscal consolidation and lower oil prices are estimated to have compressed imports over 2024-25 , helping to reduce the current account deficit to an estimated 2 . 7 percent of GDP in 2025 (Text Figure 1). The current account deficit is projected to widen in 2026 because of worsening terms of trade emanating from the war in the Middle East, notably higher energy and food prices. Exports are projected to remain at around 15 percent of GDP, supported by exports from special economic

<!-- image -->

zones and trade and travel services , following recent investments , whilst remittance inflows are expected to moderate further but stabilize above pre-pandemic levels.

Assessment. Using 2025 estimates, the EBA-lite model estimates a current account norm of -3 . 7 percent of GDP, wider than the adjusted CA balance of -3 . 2 percent of GDP. The estimation includes a standard adjustor of -0.02 percent of GDP for cyclical contributions and 0.4 percent of GDP for natural disasters/conflict, and an ad hoc adjustor of 0.02 percent of GDP to account for temporarily higher levels of remittances; lower than in the previous assessment as remittances normalize. The CA gap of 0 . 5 percent of GDP (Text Table ) suggests Togo's current account is broadly in line with the level implied by fundamentals and desirable policies. The relative policy gap explains only 0.1 percent of GDP, implying that the residual may be explained by other country-specific factors such as re -export activity and data limitations .

## Real Exchange Rate

Background. Togo is part of the WAEMU currency union, with the CF franc pegged to the euro. The country's real effective exchange rate (REER) appreciated by 3 . 8 percent over 2019 – 21 before depreciating by 2.3 percent in 2022, reflecting the nominal depreciation of the Euro after Russia's invasion of Ukraine and international monetary policy tightening. In line with the relative strengthening of the Euro and a positive inflation differential vis -à -vis trading partners, the REER appreciated by 8 . 2 percent over 2023 -25 (Text Figure 2).

Assessment. When applied to 2025 estimates, the EBA-Lite Index Real Effective Exchange Rate (IREER) model estimates a small overvaluation of 2 . 1 percent with respect to values implied by economic fundamentals and desirable policies, compared to an overvaluation of 1.0 percent in the previous

<!-- image -->

assessment. By contrast, the EBA-Lite current account model, which is generally a better fit for low-income countries, implies a small undervaluation of 2 . 7 percent under an elasticity of -0.2 .

## Capital and Financial Accounts: Flows and Policy Measures

Background. Inward capital transfers have exceeded current account deficits since 2017. This was driven by private capital transfers used for investment purposes 1 and official capital transfers that supported higher public investment. Whilst the latter is estimated to have declined since its 2023 peak, the former has continued to grow, sustaining a capital account surplus of around 5 percent of GDP in 2025, above prepandemic levels. Transactions by regional banks headquartered in Togo have resulted in swings in direct, portfolio and other investment in the financial account and explain financial account deficits in several years. Increased government external borrowing has reduced net other investment outflows, whilst reduced net issuance on the regional market partly explains projections of net portfolio outflows in the medium term .

Assessment. Staff does not find immediate risks or

<!-- image -->

vulnerabilities linked to capital flows. Whilst moderating, capital transfers are expected to continue to exceed the current account deficit even with the current energy shock. Fiscal discipline is expected to contain borrowing needs whilst the scale -up of borrowing from non-WAEMU creditors should shift the composition of government flows from shorter-term portfolio flows related to non-Togolese bank holdings of Togo's securities to longer-term other investment in the medium term. The authorities should continue to improve the business environment and investment climate to increase and diversity inward FDI beyond the financial sector.

## Reserves Level

Background. Togo is a currency union member with reserves pooled at the WAEMU level and the same is true for policies on foreign exchange intervention, monetary operations, and capital account openness. The assessment , therefore, refers to that in the 2026 Staff Report on Common Policies for Member Countries . WAEMU pooled reserves recovered from about $16 billion (3.5 months of prospective imports) at end-2023 to about $41 billion at end-2025 (7.0 months of prospective imports) . The 2025 buildup reflects the highest trade balance in more than a decade, driven also by favorable terms of trade and the ramp-up in hydrocarbon production. As a share of prospective imports, reserves are projected to rise smoothly over the medium term, reflecting sustained exports of W EMU's main commodities.

Assessment. The adequate level of WAEMU reserves is assessed to lie between 5 . 0 and 7 . 1 months of prospective import coverage. End-2025 data indicate that reserves were within these levels , while medium -term projections (8 . 1 months of prospective imports by 2030 are above them. Growth-friendly fiscal consolidation, appropriate monetary policy, and implementation of structural reforms remain key to sustaining reserves at adequate levels going forward .

1 The BCEAO indicated that it classifies some private transfers as capital, rather than current, where there is an indication that they are used to finance investment (e.g. in real estate) . IMF TA suggested that such transfers are current transfers . Granular data to separate such transfers is not currently available but, including them in the current account, would clearly reduce the deficit further and may indicate that the external position is stronger than the current assessment.

## Annex III . Illustrative Downside Scenario: Global Energy Prices Higher for Longer

1. This annex considers a downside scenario in which global fuel prices remain significantly above the baseline in 2026 – 27, representing a significant negative terms-of-trade shock for Togo. The shock is consistent with the pril 202 World Economic Outlook " evere" scenario, which assumes an average petroleum spot price of about US$112/barrel in 2026 and US$122/barrel in 202 . The macroeconomic impact of this shock depends critically on the authorities' pricing policy response.
2. The analysis presents two alternative policy responses within the same shock:

- A limited pass-through of international prices to domestic pump prices, implying continued government intervention to contain price increases.
- Full pass-through, where higher international prices are fully reflected in domestic fuel prices.
- Other revenue and expenditure policies remain the same as under the baseline.

## Transmission Channels

3. The shock affects the economy through four main channels:

- Growth: Higher fuel costs reduce real incomes and raise production costs, weighing on consumption and investment. In addition, weaker global demand dampens exports, including transit trade.
- Inflation: Higher international fuel prices feed into domestic prices through pump prices or indirectly through transport and production costs. The size of the increase

4. depends on the degree of pass-through, although even with limited pass-through, higher global prices will affect imported inflation.

- Fiscal: Under limited pass-through, the budget absorbs part of the shock through higher subsidies, widening the fiscal deficit. With full pass-through, fiscal pressures are contained, although the impact of lower growth will be felt through automatic stabilizers .
- External: Togo will face a higher import bill, leading to a deterioration of the current account in both cases, with limited scope for adjustment in the short term. The impact on the current account

<!-- image -->

48

is less pronounced with full pass-through, as import demand adjusts to higher prices and lower growth.

## Macroeconomic Outcomes

4. Macroeconomic outcomes vary depending on the degree of pass-through. Limited passthrough cushions inflation and growth in the near term but leads to a significant deterioration in fiscal balances and widening of the current account deficit. Full pass-through contains fiscal pressures but results in higher inflation and a more pronounced slowdown in economic activity.

| Table 1. Togo: Results of Downside Scenario  With 15% pass-through to pump prices  Deviation from baseline  2026  2027  2026  2027  APSP oil price (US$)  112  122  21  43  Real GDP growth (percent)  4.7  4.5  -0.3  -0.8  Inflation (percent)  4.8  4.2  2.0  1.9  Fiscal balance (percent of GDP)  -4.1  -4.6  -0.7  -1.6  Current account balance (percent of GDP)  -5.0  -4.9  -0.8  -1.5  With 100% pass-through to pump prices  Deviation from baseline  2026  2027  2026  2027  APSP oil price (US$)  112  122  21  43  Real GDP growth (percent)  4.0  3.7  -1.0  -1.6  Inflation (percent)  6.2  5.9  3.4  3.6  Fiscal balance (percent of GDP)  -2.7  -2.7  0.7  0.3  Current account balance (percent of GDP)  -4.6  -4.2  -0.4  -0.8 |
| ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |

## Policy Response

5. The downside scenario highlights the difficult policy trade-offs associated with fuel price adjustment. Under limited pass-through, fiscal costs would rise significantly, requiring sizeable offsetting measures to preserve debt sustainability and avoid additional pressure on WAEMU reserves. In practice, such adjustment would likely fall on the expenditure side, including public investment, with potential adverse implications for growth. By contrast, full pass-through would contain fiscal pressures but at the cost of higher inflation and weaker near-term activity.
6. Should the downside scenario materialize, the policy mix would need to be recalibrated to safeguard program objectives. Given limited fiscal space and debt vulnerabilities, adjustment would need to rely primarily on stronger domestic price pass-through, complemented by targeted measures to protect vulnerable households . This underscores the urgency of the development of a comprehensive and targeted social safety net .

## Annex IV . Capacity Development Strategy

Fund capacity development (CD) will support the authorities' key objectives, in line with program goals, including strengthening fiscal revenue, enhancing the social safety net, and improving public financial management. The areas identified below include past and possible future CD .

## 1. Past capacity development activities in Togo have shown relatively good traction and supported important reforms:

1. Tax and customs administrations have been strengthened in recent years. For example, the authorities have implemented several CD recommendations on tax policy delivered in 2017 . They have strengthened taxpayer registration and expanded the use of electronic processing. FAD recently supported the authorities in developing a Medium-Term Revenue Strategy, which will support the objective of raising fiscal revenues in the near and medium term.
2. The authorities have implemented important reforms of public financial management (PFM), including a transition to program budgeting, and the development of a green budget and a gender-based budget thanks in part to support since 2012 via a resident FAD PFM advisor financed by a series of successive EU-funded projects. The work program for the current project focuses on program and priority-based budgeting, public investment management, and fiscal risk management. Significant progress has been made, with, for example, the development of a fiscal risk statement, a monitoring dashboard for SOEs, the modernization of Public Investment Management Procedures, the reinforcement of budget execution procedures including through strengthened financial controls. The administrative process for extension to FY27 of the EU-funded PFM project has been completed. Amongst activities financed thanks to the extension of the project is support to the reorganization of the Budget Directorate.
3. Debt management has improved with support from the Fund, including through the creation of a new debt directorate and the preparation of a new procedure manual. The authorities are now preparing regular updates of their medium-term debt strategy (MTDS) and publishing quarterly debt bulletins on central government debt.
4. The National Statistics Institute (INSEED) has been strengthened, enabling a revision of Togo's national accounts in line with good practice. AFRITAC West has been very active in supporting the INSEED in the development of quarterly national accounts, and more recently, on providing support for the GDP rebasing initiated by the authorities.
5. Fund CD provision will support key government objectives:

- Raising fiscal revenue through tax policy and tax administration reforms . FAD should continue providing CD on a range of select topics, such as strengthening taxation of property or mining activities, supporting the authority on the development of property tax,

digitalization and governance reforms in the tax administration, all of which could contribute to higher revenues.

- Strengthening PFM. CD should continue to support a strengthening of public investment management, SOE and PPP management, and fiscal reporting. Fiscal transparency reforms, digitalization of procedures, and measures to improve expenditure efficiency should also be considered. Furthermore, the authorities are seeking to establish a Medium-Term Expenditure Strategy, aligned with their revenue constraints, which could be supported by FAD . The authorities have already reached out to the FAD PFM resident advisor and requested support for the elaboration of a SOE ownership strategy. This important reform should be prioritized, to ensure enhanced governance in SOEs and transparency. Support on subsidies and public-sector pricing, which would be directly relevant for both fuel subsidy reform and utility tariff setting, could be areas of CD for FAD.
- Strengthening Togo's social safety net to make growth more inclusive. Fund CD could help the authorities determine the best way to use limited budget resources to support the most vulnerable. This could be done in coordination with work on a Medium -Term Revenue Mobilization Strategy to arrive at a comprehensive Fiscal Policy Strategy that integrates revenue generation and spending policies (see above).
- Further, CD will continue helping the authorities enhance statistical capacity. CD will continue to strengthen capacity to compile statistics on national accounts, government finance, public sector debt, and the external sector.
- In addition, Fund staff are working to provide CD on governance. Staff will prepare a report for the Governance Diagnostic Assessment mission of February 2026 . Staff could also assist in strengthening the legal and institutional framework for AML/CFT to bring it in line with FATF standards and stand ready to advise on strengthening the framework for Assets Declarations.

## Annex V. Understanding Fiscal Reporting and Debt Developments in 2025

This annex reconciles fiscal, debt, and monetary data for 2025. It first compares debt accumulation implied by fiscal flow data from the Tableau des Opérations Financières de l'État (TOFE) with changes in the public debt stock reported by the authorities' Debt Directorate. It then assesses consistency between budgetary financing flows and changes in depository corporations' net claims on government as reported in the BCEAO's monetary survey. Fiscal reporting has improved and the gap between debt accumulation and the fiscal deficit in 2025 was much smaller. The authorities have completed the fiscal-debt reconciliation report for 2025.

## A. Introduction

1. The previous staff report analyzed the drivers of the divergence between debt accumulation and the fiscal deficit in 2024. In the context of the Second Review under the ECF, staff reconciled budget execution data from the Tableau des Opérations Financières de l'État (TOFE) with debt accumulation and changes in the government's net position vis-à-vis the banking system. The analysis identified unbudgeted below-the-line operations—financed through unplanned external borrowing—as a key driver of the faster-than-expected increase in central government debt.
2. 

Building on this assessment, the present annex extends the reconciliation analysis to

2025. It examines the consistency between fiscal flows, debt accumulation, and monetary developments, and assesses recent improvements in fiscal and debt reporting, including the authorities' publication of a reconciliation between net deficit financing and changes in the public debt stock. The annex provides an updated assessment of fiscal and debt developments in 2025 and informs ongoing efforts to strengthen fiscal reporting and transparency.

## B. Reconciliation of Debt Flow and Debt Stock Data

3. Fiscal outturns and financing flows reveal gaps in coverage of the 2025 budget execution as the recorded deficit is not fully matched by identified financing flows. Based on data from the Tableau des Opérations Financières de l'État (TOFE), execution of the 2025 budget resulted in a cash -basis fiscal deficit of CF F 4. billion (2. percent of G P; Table ). Financing was provided by net debt inflows of CF F . billion ( . percent of G P) and an increase in net claims on government by the banking system of CF F 0.2 billion (0.4 percent of G P), leaving a financing gap of CF F 0. billion . 1

1 The TOFE for a given year reports (i) the execution of the budget for the calendar year (which stretches into the first two months of the following calendar year), and (ii) all below-the-line and financing operations undertaken during the calendar year. The TOFE relates to the central government and combines cash and commitment reporting: revenue is reported on a cash basis, while spending is reported mainly on a commitment basis (at the "engagement" stage), with reporting on debt related spending on a cash basis. The fact that part of the TOFE relates to commitments rather than cash spending can introduce limited unexplained residuals in the effort to establish consistency for any given year, given that disbursements from debt accumulation and changes in the government's position vis a vis the banking system are registered on a cash basis.

## 4. Below -the -line operations partly offset the financing gap, leaving a small residual. Net movements in Treasury correspondent deposit accounts, reducing the unexplained residual to CF F

below -the -line financing amounted to CF F 25. billion (0.4 percent of G P), largely reflecting 4. billion (0. percent of G P). The difference between the overall fiscal balance on a cash and commitment basis amounted to CF F .2 billion (0. percent of G P).

## 5. Debt flow and stock data for 2025 are broadly consistent once identifiable

reconciliation items are considered. Debt flow data from the TOFE indicate that net disbursements increased public debt by CFAF 133.8 billion (1.9 percent of GDP) in 2025, while debt stock data show a smaller increase of CFAF 94.7 billion (1.4 percent of GDP) over the same period. To enhance fiscal transparency, the authorities have begun publishing a reconciliation—presented in the statistical bulletin on public debt—between net deficit financing and changes in the debt stock. This reconciliation identifies a gap of CFAF 39.1 billion (about 0.5 percent of GDP), which is fully explained by identifiable factors and does not point to unexplained debt accumulation.

6. Identifiable reconciliation items fully explain the difference between debt flows and debt stock changes in 2025. The main reconciling factors include: (i) timing differences between commitment -based recording and actual cash disbursements, including lags between payment requests and disbursements (CFAF 3.3 billion); (ii) the settlement of KPMG-related liabilities and arrears of state -owned enterprises (CFAF -41.7 billion, 0.6 percent of GDP) 2 ; (iii) exchange-rate valuation effects on foreign-currency debt (CFAF -48.3 billion); and (iv) disbursements not captured by the budget balance (CF F 4 . billion). Taken together, these items yield net "other flows" of – CFAF 39.1 billion, fully accounting for the discrepancy between fiscal flows and the change in the debt stock.
7. Overall, the reconciliation confirms consistency between fiscal and debt data. There is no evidence of a material unexplained stock-flow adjustment in 2025.

## C. Reconciliation of Budget Financing and Monetary Data

8. This section assesses the consistency between budgetary financing flows and monetary developments. At end-December 2025, the TOFE records an increase in net claims on government of CFAF 61.4 billion, compared with an increase of CFAF 203.9 billion reported in the BCEAO monetary survey. The resulting discrepancy is driven primarily by differences in the recorded change in the BCE O's net claims on government (CF F .2 billion) and commercial banks' net claims on government (CFAF 71.7 billion).
