Note: PNG (position nette du gouvernement) is net claims of depository corporations on the general government and PNT (position nette du tresor) is net claims of depository corporations on the treasury (budgetary central government).

## Net Claims of the BCEAO

9. Differences between the monetary survey and the TOFE largely reflect institutional coverage, particularly in the treatment of central bank positions. At end-December 2025, the change in the BCE O's net claims on central government amounted to CF F . billion in the PNG, compared with CFAF 0.4 billion in the PNT, implying a discrepancy of CFAF 33.2 billion. This difference is driven primarily by the treatment of central government deposits at the BCEAO, which stood at CFAF 31.9 billion in the PNG but only CFAF 1.3 billion in the PNT.

## Net Claims of Commercial Banks

10. Discrepancies in commercial banks' net claims on government reflect both broader institutional coverage and differences in the treatment of financial instruments. At end -ecember 2025, commercial banks' net claims on government increased by CF F 0. billion in the PNG, compared with CFAF 61.0 billion in the PNT, resulting in a discrepancy of CFAF 109.4 billion. This difference comprises CFAF 71.7 billion on the asset side and CF F − . billion on the liability side. Commercial banks' claims on government include direct credit and holdings of government securities. In total, these claims amounted to CFAF 173.2 billion in the PNG and CFAF 101.5 billion in the PNT. The remaining difference reflects differences in both institutional coverage and the treatment of financial instruments, particularly direct credit and government securities.

## Implications

11. Overall discrepancies between monetary and fiscal data mainly reflect differences in institutional coverage between the PNG and the PNT. The monetary survey is compiled based on the broader central government perimeter (PNG), while the TOFE is limited to budgetary central government (PNT). Further improvements in data granularity are needed to strengthen the consistency between fiscal and monetary reporting . With support from Fund technical assistance, the authorities have begun identifying monetary flows attributable specifically to budgetary central government and have incorporated these data into the end-December 2025 TOFE. The authorities are working with the BCEAO to understand the reasons for this difference between the PNT and the PNG. Preliminary work points to banks mis-classifying credits to all parts of government as central government , loans/securities to other WAEMU governments as those to Togo, and mixing securities holdings with loans.
12. Going forward, strengthening the alignment between fiscal and monetary data will require continued improvements in coverage, coordination, and cash management. Continued collaboration with the BCEAO will be essential to more systematically isolate budgetary central government transactions in monetary statistics, building on current practices for commercial bank deposits. At the same time, the marked increase in recourse to bank financing in 2025—by both the Treasury and the broader general government—together with the widening gap between the PNG and the PNT in recent years, warrants close monitoring. The simultaneous accumulation of government deposits alongside increased borrowing points to scope to strengthen cash-flow forecasting and improve cash management. Over the medium term, broadening the fiscal coverage to include extra budgetary units would further improve fiscal reporting and transparency

## Appendix I . Letter of Intent

Ms. Kristalina Georgieva Managing Director International Monetary Fund Washington, DC 20431

Dear Madam Managing Director:

- On behalf of the Government of Togo, we reaffirm our strong commitment to the objectives of the economic program supported by the Extended Credit Facility (ECF) arrangement. We believe that the policies and measures outlined in the attached Memorandum of Economic and Financial Policies (MEFP), together with this Letter of Intent (LOI), remain appropriate to achieving the program's objectives.
- Program implementation has helped preserve macroeconomic stability and strengthen debt sustainability. Economic growth for 2025 is estimated at around 6½ percent, supported by robust activity in the services sector, while inflation declined to 0.4 percent (annual average), largely reflecting lower food prices. Despite shortfalls in revenue collection, fiscal consolidation remains on track, with the fiscal deficit reduced to 3.2 percent of GDP, driven particularly by expenditure restraint in goods and services and domestically financed investment. This has helped reduce our debt-to-GDP ratio to 64.9 percent . Combined with consolidation efforts across WAEMU member countries and improved terms of trade, these developments contributed to the recovery of regional international reserves, which reached 8.1 months of imports in March 2026. The external position remains broadly in line with our economic fundamentals. While the economic outlook remains favorable, it is subject to elevated risks, notably due to volatility in global oil prices, tightening financial conditions, and raising security risks.
- Regarding program performance, four out of five quantitative performance criteria (QPCs) and both indicative targets (ITs) for end June 2025 were met. The QPC on net domestic financing was not met, reflecting higher-than-anticipated borrowing from the domestic banking sector following delays in external disbursements. At end December 2025, all QPCs were met except for a small breach (0.4 percent of GDP) of the continuous performance criterion regarding the ceiling of new external borrowing. We request waivers for the nonobservance of these performance criteria as, in both cases, the deviation was of a limited size, temporary, and did not have a significant fiscal impact on the program's objectives. We also request waivers for the nonobservance (0.02 percent of GDP) in January 2026 of the continuous PCs on the nonaccumulation of external debt arrears and the ceiling on new external borrowing by 0.8 percent

Lomé, June 9 , 2026

of GDP in March 2026. The late payment, due to administrative reasons as the creditor did not submit the payment notice on time, has been cleared. The ceiling on new external borrowing for 2026 was set prior to the preparation of the 2026 budget and borrowing plan . There is also room to raise this ceiling without compromising the program's objectives. We therefore request modification for the end -June 2026 PCs on the floor on the basic primary balance , the ceiling on net domestic financing , and the ceiling on the contracting of new external debt (and its adjustor). Regarding ITs, all were met at end–December, except for the one on tax revenue, which fell short of the end -2025 floor by 0.9 percent of GDP. We are taking corrective actions, as set out in this MEFP, to ensure convergence toward program targets for this IT in the future.

- We have made meaningful progress in advancing the structural reform agenda. Seven out of eight structural benchmarks were met since the completion of the second review. In particular, we have adopted a ministerial order requiring all state-owned enterprises (SOEs) to produce financial statements to be annexed to the fiscal risk statement. We also strengthened the anticorruption framework, mandating the publication of beneficial ownership information in public procurement. While the report on identifying the drivers of unexplained debt accumulation was submitted timely, additional time was required to incorporate comments and finalize the analysis. Nonetheless, fiscal and debt reporting have improved markedly, notably thanks to enhanced accounting and reporting systems .
- In the banking sector, progress was hindered by the results of an on-site inspection by the WAEMU Banking Commission, requested by the government. The inspection revealed that the asset quality of a financial institution was significantly weaker than previously assessed, hence indicating that the institution remains largely undercapitalized despite our previous efforts. In light of these developments, the end-2024 structural benchmark on raising capital to the regulatory minimum of CFAF 10 billion was not met, contrary to earlier assessments in the context of the second review of the program. The Banking Commission's report was transmitted to the institution in May 2025 and was followed by a contradictory review at end-June 2025. The Banking Commission's final injunction was issued in early July, which explains the transmission of the report to IMF Staff after the Board meeting. However, to prevent the recurrence of such situations in the future and to ensure a proper monitoring of the program, we commit to promptly providing any information that could affect the program's evaluation. We also reaffirm our full commitment to implementing all necessary measures to resolve the institution based on the results of the asset quality review.
- The 2026 energy shock is expected to place significant pressure on our public finances. To help preserve macroeconomic stability and contain these pressures, we will undertake a fiscal adjustment. This adjustment will reflect the impact of the energy shock on the level of subsidies while reducing non-priority spending and strengthening targeted support to vulnerable populations exposed to rising energy prices. Despite these constraints, we remain firmly committed to achieving the program's objectives and preserving macroeconomic stability. To this end, the fiscal deficit is expected to be contained at 3.4 percent of GDP in 2026. While this temporarily delays the achievement of the regional target, we remain fully committed to converging the community norm of 3 percent of GDP.
- Looking ahead, continued reform efforts are essential to preserve macroeconomic stability, maintain debt sustainability, promote inclusive growth, and reduce poverty. Since October 2025, we have been working on a new National Development Plan (2026-30), for which the consultation with the main stakeholders was achieved mid -May 2026 and we are committed to completing it as soon as possible. To this end, we request presenting this new strategy at the fifth review. In the meantime, the 2020 -25 roadmap will continue to serve as the main policy anchor. We will strengthen domestic revenue mobilization through the adoption of a mediumterm revenue strategy (MTRS) and the implementation of new tax policy and revenue administration measures, with the objective of increasing tax revenue by at least 0.5 percent of GDP per year. We will continue to strengthen public financial management, improve spending efficiency, and mitigate fiscal risks, particularly those related to SOEs.
- The attached MEFP describes progress under the ECF arrangement and sets out our economic policies for the period ahead. We believe that these policies are adequate to achieve the program's objectives and remain ready to take additional measures as needed. We will consult with IMF staff in advance of any policy revisions and will provide all information required for program monitoring, as specified in the Technical Memorandum of Understanding (TMU).
- We do not intend to introduce measures that would exacerbate balance of payments difficulties or that would be inconsistent with rticle VIII of the IMF's rticles of greement, nor do we intend to accumulate external or domestic arrears.
- On the basis of our overall policy implementation and continued commitment to the reform agenda, we request completion of the combined third and fourth reviews under the ECF arrangement and the associated disbursement of SDR 80.74 million (55 percent of quota). In view of the temporary nature of the deviations, the changed circumstances, and the corrective measures taken, we also request the Executive Board's approval of requested waivers of nonobservance of the relevant performance criteria, as discussed above .

The Government of Togo consents to the publication of the IMF staff report, this letter, the attached MEFP, and the TMU.

Please accept, Madam Managing Director, the assurance of our highest consideration.

/s/

Essowè Georges Barcola Minister of Finance and Budget

## Attachment I. Memorandum of Economic and Financial Policies

Growth prospects are favorable, but development needs remain large , and risks are elevated. With the support of the IMF under the ECF-arrangement approved in March 2024, we intend to (i) make growth more inclusive while improving debt sustainability; and (ii) implement structural reforms to support growth and limit fiscal and financial sector risks.

## I. BACKGROUND

1. Our economy , supported by fiscal measures, has shown resilience in the face of recent shocks, yet development challenges persist alongside ongoing security threats. While economic growth has remained resilient, food insecurity remains elevated, affecting at least 22.6 percent of the population 1 . Significant gaps persist with respect to several Sustainable Development Goals. At the same time, the security situation along the northern border remains fragile, and climate-related shocks are increasingly weighing on economic activity. This year, we updated the macroeconomic framework to reflect the latest final national accounts for 2020 – 23, which raised the level of nominal GDP . Recent estimates also point to stronger growth in 2024 and 2025, at around 6½ percent in both years, respectively, mechanically reducing fiscal and debt ratios, all else equal .
2. The fiscal expansion implemented in response to past shocks helped mitigate their impact but also led to higher fiscal deficits and public debt, requiring substantial consolidation. In 2022, the fiscal deficit peaked at 8.3 percent of GDP, driven by measures aimed at preserving households' purchasing power, including significant fuel subsidies. By 202 , even before the launch of the ECF -supported program, the deficit was reduced to 6.7 percent of GDP, largely reflecting the scaling back of fuel subsidies.
3. In response to elevated fiscal deficits and rising public debt, we adopted an ambitious program of economic and financial policies in 2024, which remains broadly appropriate. The program aims to preserve macroeconomic stability and accelerate i) poverty reduction by promoting inclusive growth while strengthening debt sustainability and ii) advancing structural reforms to support growth and mitigate fiscal and financial sector risks. Key policy priorities include scaling up social spending for vulnerable groups and enhancing social safety nets, implementing a large fiscal consolidation supported by ambitious domestic revenue mobilization, advancing public financial management and governance reforms, and completing needed reforms of public financial institutions. The International Monetary Fund is supporting our efforts through an Extended Credit Facility arrangement approved in March 2024, providing access equivalent to 200 percent of quota (SDR 293.60 million) over a total duration of 53 months (extended from an initial 42 months) .

1 The data on food insecurity are sourced from the Harmonized Framework Harmonisé | IPC - Integrated Food Security Phase Classification).

<!-- image -->

## II. RECENT ECONOMIC DEVELOPMENTS

4. Economic growth remained resilient in 2025 and is now estimated at about 6½ percent, supported by strong activity in the logistics sector. Following robust growth of around 6½ percent in 2024, economic expansion slowed slightly but remains solid, reflecting increased port and airport traffic as well as expanded handling and container capacity. Growth estimate for 2025 was revised upward from 5.2 to around 6½ percent, driven by stronger-than-anticipated performance in the services sector. Headline inflation (year-on-year, end of period) moderated to 0.7 percent in April 2026, driven mainly by lower food prices, but this does not yet reflect the impact of rising international energy prices.

## 5. The external position remained broadly in line with economic fundamentals and

desirable policies in 2025. Preliminary estimates indicate that the current account deficit widened slightly to 2.7 percent of GDP. Increased port traffic, much of which is handled by non-resident freight service providers, led to a decline in net service exports, offsetting a smaller goods trade deficit, which was driven mainly by lower imports . Export performance remained subdued, as prices for some export commodities, notably phosphate, moderated. However, regional foreign exchange reserves rebounded to 8.1 months of imports in March 2026, well above adequacy thresholds, partly reflecting improved terms of trade.

## 6. Fiscal policy stayed anchored to program objectives, despite weaker-than-expected

revenue performance. Tax revenues rose by 3.9 percent year-on-year but reached 93.5 percent of the 2025 target, reducing the tax-to-GDP ratio by about 0.4 percentage points relative to 2024, notably due to increased tax expenditures and partial implementation of the 2025 revenue mobilization plan. Nontax revenues (as a share of GDP) were slightly lower than in the previous year, which had benefited from one -off operations, including the collection of dividends from earlier periods . Owing to expenditure restraint—particularly on goods and services and domestically financed capital spending—the overall fiscal deficit was significantly reduced from 6.8 percent of GDP in 2024 to 3.2 percent of GDP in 2025, reflecting performance exceeding program targets. Notwithstanding this performance, outstanding payment obligations equivalent to 0.6 percent of GDP were accumulated due to end -year financing constraints.

7. Sustained fiscal consolidation contributed to a decline in the public debt-to-GDP ratio and helped maintain a moderate risk of external debt distress, although buffers to absorb shocks remain limited in the short term. As part of our medium-term debt strategy, we continued to prioritize long-term concessional external loans and mobilized guarantees from the AfDB and the World Bank Group to reduce borrowing costs and alleviate refinancing risks. We have also taken on more long-term non-concessional external debt. In addition, we have concluded procedures to expunge from the debt portfolio historic domestic arrears for which no claims have been forthcoming. Overall , public debt declined to about 64.9 percent of GDP by end-2025 and the share of domestic debt also decreased. Debt remains sustainable despite near-term liquidity pressures, although buffers to absorb shocks remain limited in the near term .
8. Vulnerabilities persist in the financial sector , which remains broadly sound . By end of December, nonperforming loans increased significantly to about 13 percent of total credit, up from 8.1 percent in September 2025, alongside a sharp rise in restructured loans. Regarding IB Bank, its financial position improved markedly, with regulatory capital turning positive by end-2025, reflecting a reduction in related party exposures and the sale of its headquarters to a private entity.

## III. OUTLOOK AND RISKS

9. Macroeconomic performance is projected to moderate in the near future . Growth is projected to slow while remaining strong at about 5 percent in 2026, reflecting mostly spillovers from the war in the Middle East. It is projected to recover gradually to about 5½ percent over the medium term, as the effects from the war dissipate . Over the medium term, increased public investment — supported by stronger revenue mobilization combined with productivity-enhancing structural reforms — is expected to bolster growth . Headline inflation is expected to increase to an annual average of 2.8 percent in 2026 , driven by higher energy prices related to the war and pressures on food prices, before easing toward the BCE O's 2-percent target. The current account deficit is projected to widen to about 4 to 4½ percent of GDP in 2026, remaining above previous projections and reflecting weaker terms of trade, notably from higher near-term energy prices.
10. The outlook remains subject to elevated downside risks. Security risks in the North, geopolitical tensions, and exposure to climate shocks remain elevated and could disrupt logistics, trade, and economic activity . Rising and volatile energy and agricultural input prices could increase inflation, weaken food security, and heighten social and fiscal pressures . Persistent vulnerabilities in the financial sector and pressure on the external balance remain. Over the longer term, debt sustainability will depend on maintaining fiscal discipline and prudent borrowing management.

## IV. PROGRAM IMPLEMENTATION

11. Performance against quantitative criteria has been broadly satisfactory , but the third review was delayed due to the need to recalibrate a credible strategy for a bank . For the third review, we met the quantitative performance criterion (QPC) on the basic primary fiscal balance, all continuous performance criteria on the present value of external debt, the non-accumulation of external arrears, and the avoidance of prefinancing arrangements, as well as the indicative targets (IT) on tax revenue and social and pro-poor spending. The only missed QPC was the ceiling on net domestic financing (by 0.32 percent of GDP), reflecting higher-than-expected borrowing from the domestic banking system due to delays in the disbursement of some external financing, including the ECF disbursement. For the fourth review, all QPCs were met except for the continuous PC on the ceiling on new external borrowing, which was exceeded (by 0.4 percent of GDP) at end-2025 . The IT on social and pro-poor spending was met. However, the IT on tax revenue was missed with a shortfall of 6.5 percent, equivalent to 0.9 percent of GDP. In addition, two continuous QPCs were missed in 2026. External payment arrears to a Paris Club creditor amounting to CFAF 1.0 billion or 0.02 percent of GDP were accumulated in January 2026 because of administrative delays and were cleared in March 2026. The continuous QPC on the contracting of new external debt was missed by 0.8 percent of GDP in March 2026, reflecting changed circumstances and greater space below DSA thresholds that allowed higher borrowing without compromising debt sustainability or program objectives.

## 12.

## Performance against structural benchmarks has remained strong since the completion

of the second review. We met all but one structural benchmarks (SBs) due since the second review. As previously reported, SB 5.3 due at end June 2025 was met through the inclusion of state-owned enterprises and government-guaranteed debt in the quarterly debt bulletin. We also met SB 4.1 due at end June 2025 by preparing an analysis of VAT exemptions to distinguish between progressive and regressive measures. Of the four structural benchmarks due at end December, three were met: we prepared a report on the taxation of civil servants to assess scope for adjustment (SB 4.2), issued an order mandating the regular production of audited financial statements for all SOEs (SB 5.1), and adopted an order requiring the publication of beneficial ownership information for companies awarded public procurement contracts (SB 6.1). In addition, we adopted in March 2026 a ministerial decree eliminating VAT exemptions equivalent to at least 0.25 percent of GDP (SB 8.1, due end March 2026). However, SB 9.1 was implemented with delay. While a report explaining the drivers of the previously unexplained debt accumulation over 2017–24 was prepared on time, additional time was needed to develop an operational strategy to prevent the recurrence of such discrepancies. Notwithstanding this delay, we undertook substantial efforts to address the factors underlying the past discrepancies and have significantly strengthened debt recording and reconciliation practices.

13. We acknowledge the need to ensure timely availability and sharing of information for program monitoring . The Banking Commission's findings on a financial institution were transmitted in a report to the institution in May 2025 and indicated that its capital remained well below regulatory requirements even though it remains viable. The report was subject to a contradictory review at end -June 2025, and the Commission's final injunction was issued in early July 2025, explaining the transmission of the report to IMF staff after the Board meeting. In light of this new information, we acknowledge that the benchmark, initially considered met, was in fact not met. To prevent the recurrence of such situations in the future and to ensure proper program monitoring, we commit to promptly providing all information that could affect the evaluation of the program.

## V. MACROECONOMIC POLICIES AND STRUCTURAL REFORMS

## A. Strengthening Public Debt Sustainability While Promoting Inclusive Growth

## Short -Term Fiscal Policy – Managing the Energy Shock

14. The 2026 energy shock is expected to exert significant pressures on public finances, and we are implementing measures to mitigate its impact. The average CIF price of new fuel imports has nearly doubled following the intensification of the war in the Middle East, which could generate an additional fiscal cost estimated at about 1 percent of GDP. To mitigate this impact, we have increased pump prices since May 26, 2026 3 . We will continue to adjust prices gradually in line with international market developments to bring the cumulative adjustment to at least 0.3 percent of GDP by end-2026. These pump price adjustment measures will be complemented by a rationalization of non -priority spending amounting to 0.5 percent of GDP. Residual subsidies (0.2 percent of GDP), reflecting a differentiated increase in pump prices to protect vulnerable groups, will be accommodated through a temporary widening of the fiscal deficit.
15. To preserve macroeconomic stability and contain these fiscal pressures, we adopted a presidential budget decree (décret d'avance) on June 8, 2026, to be followed in due course by a supplementary budget law. This decree will aim to contain the fiscal deficit at 3.4 percent of GDP, compared with 4.0 percent in the absence of measures and an initial target of 3 percent. The advance decree will open additional appropriations corresponding to the deterioration of the budget deficit relative to the initial target, in order to account for the effects of the shock on petroleum product prices and to strengthen targeted support mechanisms for vulnerable populations. In connection with this advance decree, the Government, in accordance with the Organic Budget Law, will initiate expenditure reallocations to reflect the impact of the energy shock on subsidies, while reducing non-priority spending—particularly capital expenditure—to be ratified in the forthcoming supplementary budget law. The limited increase in the deficit (0.4 percentage points of GDP) reflects energy subsidies arising from the differentiated price adjustment (0.2 percent of GDP), targeted support measures for vulnerable populations (about 0.1 percent of GDP), as well as the impact of slower growth acting as an automatic stabilizer (0.1 percent of GDP). We remain committed to resuming the fiscal consolidation path as the shock subsides.

## 16. We are strengthening social protection mechanisms and enhancing communication

efforts to support the adjustment and maintain reform momentum. With support from the World Bank, we will accelerate the finalization of the unified social registry of individuals and households (RSPM), whose implementation will begin by end-October 2027, to enable more effectively targeted transfers. In the interim, we will accelerate progress toward launching a pilot phase by December 2026 (RS 13.1, new structural benchmark, end December 2026). Price adjustments will be accompanied by proactive, high-level communication aimed at explaining their necessity in the context of the shock and outlining the mitigation measures to protect vulnerable households.

17. Fiscal policy in 2026 will further strengthen domestic resource mobilization to support fiscal consolidation and create space for priority spending. In this context, revenues are expected to increase by 0.5 percent of GDP, from 13.2 percent of GDP in 2025 to 13.7 percent in 2026, mainly driven by tax policy measures (about 0.4 percentage points of GDP), supported by strengthened tax administration. Key measures included in the budget comprise the introduction of new taxes on certain agricultural exports (soybeans, cashew nuts, shea), taxation of winnings above CFAF 500,000 paid to bettors, and VAT on digital services, for which the legal framework has been defined and is

3 We increased prices on May 26 th (effective May 27 th ): gasoline increased by 7 percent, kerosene increased by 60 percent and diesel increased by 8 percent.

64

being operationalized to clarify scope and territoriality rules. In addition, in March 2026 we adopted a decree eliminating VAT exemptions, including those introduced during the COVID-19 pandemic, with an estimated yield of at least 0.25 percent of GDP (end-March 2026 SB, met). Property taxation will also be strengthened. These measures are complemented by enhanced tax and customs administration, notably through certified e-invoicing, intensified audits, and accelerated recovery of tax arrears. We will closely monitor revenue performance and take corrective measures as needed, including through the adoption of a supplementary budget law.

18. On the expenditure side, we are balancing the need to mitigate the energy shock by strengthening social protection and preserving priority spending. Total expenditure is expected to increase by 0.3 percent of GDP, driven by higher energy-related spending (+0.7 percent of GDP) and targeted transfers to vulnerable households (+0.1 percent of GDP), partly offset by savings of about -0.5 percent of GDP from the rationalization of non-priority expenditure, including the postponement of public investment reflecting execution rates in previous years. Social spending will be safeguarded, particularly through the continuation of the emergency program in the Savanes region affected by insecurity, to improve access to basic services. Accordingly, program targets for social spending are maintained.
19. Given the high degree of uncertainty, we stand ready to take additional measures to achieve program objectives while protecting vulnerable households if the shock proves larger than expected. In this context, we will further adjust domestic fuel prices and provide targeted support to vulnerable populations, including leveraging the social registry (RSPM) currently under development.

## Medium -Term Fiscal Policy

20. Despite the short-term impact of the energy shock, we remain fully committed to medium -term fiscal consolidation to ensure debt sustainability while protecting pro-poor and growth-enhancing spending. Reducing the fiscal deficit to 3 percent of GDP by 2027, in line with program objectives and our commitments under WAEMU, as well as maintaining a moderate risk of debt distress, remain our key fiscal objectives.
21. The forthcoming Medium-Term Revenue Strategy (MTRS) will guide our revenue mobilization efforts. Preparatory work to finalize the MTRS (SB 8.3, initially scheduled for end-June 2026 and proposed to be extended to end-September 2026) is progressing with IMF technical assistance, and an extension of the timeline has been requested to ensure the quality and consistency of reforms. Following its adoption, we will identify measures to increase revenues by at least 0.5 percent of GDP per year (SB 8.4, end-December 2026), raising the tax-to-GDP ratio from 13.7 percent in 2026 to 15.7 percent by 2030.
22. Our medium -term expenditure framework will be aligned with the revenue mobilization strategy. Current spending is expected to gradually decline, while public investment will increase in line with improved revenue mobilization. Social and pro-poor spending will continue to rise, notably under the emergency program for the Savanes region. We will continue ongoing

efforts to improve the efficiency of public spending, including through the digitalization of public procurement.

## Debt Management

## 23. We will continue to pursue prudent debt management, prioritizing external

concessional borrowing . In line with our Medium -Term Debt Strategy, we are gradually rebalancing our portfolio towards longer-term external debt at lower interest rates. To this end, we signed new external loans in early 2026 and reduced issuances on the regional market until March. To reflect evolving conditions in the regional market and our broader financing options, we are committed to revising our MTDS. In the meantime, we will continue to prioritize concessional loans and avoid taking on further short-term external debt, taking into account the elevated debt service costs in 2026 -27, and carefully weighing the costs and risks of non-concessional external debt, particularly vis -a-vis regional issuance, where conditions have eased recently. We will also continue to explore refinancing options for maturing short-term debt.

24. We have made progress in expunging the remaining stock of historical domestic arrears and the debt of liquidated SOEs. We implemented the "four-year forfeiture" procedure to extinguish most of the small stock of historic arrears to suppliers (CFAF 5.5 billion) for which no claims have been forthcoming. For the debt of liquidated SOEs (CFAF 36.2 billion), due diligence is underway to assess its legal basis, and a decree is being prepared for their removal from the debt portfolio .

## B. Strengthening Public Debt Sustainability While Promoting Inclusive Growth

Strengthening the Banking System

## 25. Recent developments at a financial institution have underscored persistent financial

vulnerabilities. At the government's request, an on-site inspection conducted by the WAEMU Banking Commission in 2025, based on data through mid-2024, revealed a substantially weaker financial position than previously assessed. In particular, non-performing loans accounted for about 57 percent of the loan portfolio at end-June 2024, while regulatory capital was estimated to be negative, at around 3 percent of GDP. In response, the Commission issued corrective measures, which we have begun to implement. In particular, we have strengthened the institution's governance and operations through the appointment of an experienced senior manager, the establishment of a new supervisory board composed of recognized experts from the private banking sector, and the adoption of updated guidelines for risk management, including credit risk. In parallel, steps have been taken to improve the balance sheet, including the transfer of selected non-performing loans, loan reclassifications, and limited adjustments to provisioning. Notwithstanding these actions, we recognize that the reliability of the financial information needs to be confirmed through an independent and credible external audit. Consequently, we recently commissioned an asset quality review for the bank, the result of which will guide future decisions, as described below.

26. Based on the results of the asset quality review, we commit to ensuring a transparent and well -sequenced credible strategy, while safeguarding financial stability. As a first step, we will complete the AQR by end-October 2026 (SB 12.1, end-October 2026). Based on the AQR results, we will choose the strategy to be pursued by end-November 2026 (SB 12.2). The selected strategy and its implementation will constitute structural benchmarks for the sixth review, while key implementation milestones will be monitored under the seventh review, to ensure a timely and effective resolution while containing fiscal and financial risks.

## 27. We are also committed to improving prudent credit provision through the banking

system. We will continue implementing measures to facilitate the production and availability of information required for credit allocation and will support the banking Commission's efforts to strengthen compliance with regulatory requirements. We will also continue efforts to improve the operating environment of financial institutions while ensuring strict adherence to prudential requirements. These measures are essential to strengthen the resilience of the banking sector and sustainably build public confidence.

## Making Growth More Inclusive

28. We are gradually implementing our social policies. For example, with support from the World Bank, we are implementing the flagship social safety nets and economic empowerment program under the National Non-Contributory Social Protection Program. Over the next three years, we aim to provide cash-for-work transfers and economic empowerment measures to 135,000 vulnerable youth, as well as unconditional cash transfers to 60,000 households with no capacity to work (including persons with disabilities and the elderly).

## 29. To enhance the impact of social policies, we will undertake a gradual and targeted

increase in social spending. We will continue to expand social spending, and more broadly spending toward vulnerable households, as a share of total expenditure, in line with the fiscal space created under the program, while preserving fiscal sustainability. We are continuing to modernize the social protection system by strengthening targeting mechanisms and improving transparency. Building on biometric identification of beneficiaries and with World Bank support, we are finalizing the unified social registry of individuals and households (RSPM). A pilot phase is expected to start by end -December 2026 (new structural benchmark). This registry will serve as a key tool to improve beneficiary identification and enable the rollout of targeted cash transfers, particularly in response to shocks, including in the current context. At the same time, we will strengthen monitoring, evaluation, and reporting systems to ensure effective targeting, efficient execution, and sustained protection of social spending

Improve Public Financial Management, Including Oversight and Management of SOEs

30. We are strengthening our efforts to identify and address the factors behind faster-thanexpected accumulation of public debt . O Over the period 2017–24, public debt increased by an average of 0.9 percent of GDP per year beyond what can be explained by standard debt -accumulation drivers. After publishing a reconciliation note on deviations in the 2024 figures, we prepared a report analyzing the factors underlying the unexplained debt accumulation over 2017 – 24 in December 2025. However, additional time was needed to design and operationalize a credible strategy to prevent such discrepancies going forward and the report was only finalized in May 2026. As a result, the structural benchmark (end December 2025, SB 9.1) was not met. Nevertheless, we have already made progress in implementing recommendations, notably through the monthly publication in the fiscal table (TOFE) of net claims of commercial banks on the central government (PNT), monthly reconciliation exercises, and the inclusion in the end-2025 public debt bulletin of an analysis of stock-flow adjustments (SB, end-June 2026). Going forward, we will publish a quarterly report on government financial operations, including a detailed explanation of discrepancies between the Treasury net position (PNT) in the fiscal data (TOFE) and the government net position (PNG) reported by the BCEAO (modified SB 9.2, by end-June 2026). We also commit to systematically publishing reconciliation between "above-the-line" and "below-the-line" operations in quarterly budget execution reports. Finally, to align fiscal reporting with international best practices, we are considering the full adoption of GFSM 2014, including improved treatment of debt costs, and have initiated preparatory work with IMF technical assistance.

## 31. We will improve oversight and management of SOEs and further strengthen the public-private partnership (PPP) framework to reduce fiscal risks, increase budget discipline , and improve service delivery.

- Regarding SOEs , we have made considerable progress in strengthening governance and supervision. In April 2025, we adopted a new legal framework governing the oversight and management of public enterprises. We expanded the scope of the quarterly debt bulletin to include SOEs and government-guaranteed debt (SB 5.3). Building on these reforms, we have initiated work toward adopting a state ownership policy aligned with the new legal framework . We also adopted legislation requiring the regular production of audited financial statements for all SOEs (by end December 2025, SB 5.1, met) and are taking steps to professionalize SOE management. To enhance monitoring, we have established a performance dashboard currently covering 21 SOEs
- For PPP, following the adoption of a new PPP law in 2021 and its implementing decree, a PPP coordinating unit—whose institutional framework has already been defined—is being operationalized. n initial project pipeline has been identified based on the authorities' 2020–25 roadmap and will be updated under the new 2026–2031 roadmap currently under finalization 4 . Furthermore, we will continue efforts to strengthen institutional and supervisory frameworks, in particular by integrating the assessment of fiscal risks associated with all PPPs into the budgetary process.

4 The government, appointed in October 2025, started the assessment of the 2020-25 roadmap in early January 2026 to support the preparation of our 2026-2031, with support from different international partners. The costing of this roadmap is being finalized and is monitored by the Ministry of Planning. While we are committed to completing this roadmap as soon as possible . We believe that it is important to take sufficient time to fully integrate the roadmap with the macroeconomic framework. In the meantime, the 2020– 2025 Roadmap, supported by the ECF, will continue to serve as the main policy anchor .

## Electricity Sector Reform

## 32. The electricity sector remains a significant source of fiscal risk, reflecting persistent

structural weaknesses in the financial position of CEET. CEET continues to record substantial losses due to tariffs below cost -recovery levels and high technical and commercial losses. Recent estimates indicate losses of CFAF 25 billion (0.4 percent of GDP) in 2025, while its debt stood at about US$550 million (5.2 percent of GDP) at end-2024, nearly 52 percent of which has been on-lent by the government. The gap between tariffs and cost recovery was estimated at 22 percent in 2024. To address CEET's difficult financial position, we increased tariff by on average 2.5 percent in 2025, while protecting the most vulnerable households.

## 33. To restore CEET's financial viability and contain fiscal risks, we will implement a

sequenced reform agenda. In the short term, we will clear arrears to independent power producers and establish a reform coordination mechanism. Under the World Bank -supported program to strengthen CEET's governance and operations, we will complete and publish a comprehensive institutional, financial, technical, and operational diagnostic (structural benchmark, end-March 2027). In parallel, we will implement measures to reduce costs and improve operational and commercial performance. Based on the diagnostic, we will adopt a time-bound performance contract with CEET which will include explicit targets, milestones and actions to ensure the implementation of priority reforms. Over the medium term, reforms will focus on introducing a multi-year cost-reflective tariff framework with automatic adjustment mechanisms, strengthening sector governance, and improving planning.

## Improving Governance and Business Climate

34. We have made progress in strengthening governance and transparency. We requested an IMF Governance Diagnostic (GD) Assessment, with missions conducted in August 2025 and January– February 2026, and we commit to publishing the GD report by end-February 2027 (SB 10.1, initially planned for December 2026) . Its findings will inform a comprehensive strategy to further strengthen economic governance and anticorruption efforts. In the meantime , we adopted a ministerial decree mandating the collection and publication of information on the companies awarded public contracts, including their names and nationalities (end December 2025, SB 6.1, met). We have also enhanced transparency through the publication of the OTR's (Togolese Revenue Authority) multiyear strategic plan, taxpayer satisfaction surveys, and taxpayer statistics. In addition, with respect to the judicial system, we intend to update the legal framework to enable the digitalization of commercial dispute resolution, with a view to improving efficiency and processing times. These measures will strengthen transparency, support revenue mobilization, and improve public expenditure management.
35. We recognize risks related to anti-money laundering and combating the financing of terrorism (AML/CFT) . To address these risks, Parliament adopted in February 2026 a new AML/CFT law aligned with the 2023 WAEMU Directive, strengthening compliance with FATF standards. The law enhances the counter -terrorism financing framework, strengthens targeted financial sanctions , establishes an Asset Recovery Agency, strengthens oversight of nonprofit organizations, and extends the AML/CFT framework to virtual asset service providers, fintech companies, and other designated nonfinancial businesses and professions. We acknowledge that implementation challenges remain and commit to strengthening the capacity of the financial intelligence unit (CENTIF) and the judiciary, fully operationalizing the beneficial ownership registry, and extending AML/CFT supervision to higher risk nonfinancial sectors, including mining and real estate as well as legal and advisory professions.
36. With support from the World Bank, we are implementing a reform program to improve the business climate through digitalization. In 2024, we automated the issuance of environmental permits. We also launched a digital platform for commercial dispute resolution and will update the legal framework to ensure its effective implementation and improve efficiency and processing times. Enhancements to the electronic public procurement platform are ongoing, while improvements to the Port of omé's trade information portal have enhanced the fluidity of trade operations.
37. We will continue our reform efforts to improve the business environment. We will build on the country's key strengths, notably the airport's role as a regional hub and the attractiveness of the Port of Lomé as a logistics platform. At the same time, we recognize that delayed payment obligations by the government to suppliers, costly and unreliable electricity, and weak road infrastructure remain key constraints for firms, particularly SMEs. We will therefore continue efforts to avoid payment delays, strengthen the reliability and financial viability of the electricity sector, modernize transport infrastructure, and promote greater formalization of the informal sector. We will also continue to facilitate greater participation of domestic firms in investment initiatives, including in the Adétikopé Industrial Platform, and foster inclusive, formal, private sector-led growth.

## PROGRAM MONITORING

38. For 2025, we request waivers for the nonobservance of the quantitative PC on ceiling for net domestic financing, as well as for the continuous PC on the ceiling on newly

contracted external borrowing. The nonobservance of the net domestic financing PC for end-June (0.32 percent of GDP) reflects delays in the disbursement of external financing, including under the ECF, which led to higher-than-expected temporary reliance on domestic financing. This temporary delay was quickly resolved, and the end-December target was met. The nonobservance of the continuous PC resulted from the contracting of a loan at the end of December that led to a minor and limited breach of the ceiling on the present value of newly contracted external debt, equivalent to 0.4 percent of GDP (a minor amount). As the loan was not disbursed at the time of signing, it did not affect our 2025 fiscal position.

39. For 2026, we also request waivers for the non-observance of two continuous PCs related to the non -accumulation of external arrears and the ceiling on new external

borrowing. A repayment equivalent to 0.02 percent of GDP (minor amount) related to a loan from a Paris Club creditor was made shortly after the contractual due date (January 2026), due to the nonreceipt of the payment notice from the lender. We have updated our internal procedures to prevent recurrence of such situations. The ceiling on new external borrowing contracted in 2026 was exceeded by 0.8 percent of GDP by end-March, due to loans contracted under our 2026 budget and medium -term debt strategy. This ceiling had been set based on the Debt Sustainability Analysis (DSA) conducted at the time of the second review and an indicative financing plan. Since then, strong fiscal performance and declining debt have created space in the DSA, allowing for increased reliance on external borrowing. We, therefore, request a waiver for the nonobservance of this criterion on the basis that the miss was minor considering these changed circumstances , and a revision of the ceiling for the remainder of 2026 to accommodate our residual borrowing plan . This does not undermine program objectives. We further commit to consulting IMF staff in advance of any new loan signing once 80 percent of the revised ceiling has been reached, to ensure compliance with the modified criterion.

## 40. We are also requesting changes to five structural benchmarks

In light of developments related to our public bank and the adoption of a new sequenced strategy , we request the deletion of the following two structural benchmarks:

SB. 7.2: "In consultation with Fund staff, adopt a plan for the restructuring of the remaining public bank" (end- March 2026), and:

SB 7.3 "implement the plan for the restructuring of the remaining public bank (end ecember 2026).

In view of the additional efforts required to finalize the medium-term revenue mobilization strategy and to take into account institutional timelines, we request that B . " dopt a Medium-term Revenue Mobilization trategy," originally scheduled for end-June 2026, be postponed to endSeptember 2026 and that its completion be formalized through a ministerial order issued by the Minister of Finance.

We propose to modify SB 9.2 as follows: for 2025, (i) publish stock-flow adjustments in the debt statistical bulletin; and (ii) publish a report on government financial operations, including an explanation of the discrepancy between the Treasury net position (PNT) in the fiscal data (TOFE) and the government net position (PNT) as reported by BCEAO.

Given the time required to review and publish the Governance Diagnostic Assessment (SB 10.1), we request that its deadline be extended to end-February 2027.

41. We will take all measures needed to meet quantitative targets and observe structural benchmarks under the ECF program. The ECF program will be subject to semiannual reviews, based on performance criteria, indicative targets, structural benchmarks and reform measures as set out in Tables 1 and 2 of this Memorandum and defined in the attached Technical Memorandum of Understanding (which also sets out the requirements for data reporting to IMF staff). The fifth ECF

program review will be based on the end-June 2026 targets . The sixth program review will be based on the end -December 2026 targets. The seventh ECF program review will be based on the end-June 2027 targets.

## Attachment II. Technical Memorandum of Understanding

This Technical Memorandum of Understanding (TMU) sets out understandings regarding assumptions and definitions of performance criteria (PCs), indicative targets (ITs), and structural benchmarks (SBs) to be applied under Togo's program supported by an Extended Credit Facility (ECF) arrangement approved in March 2024. It also specifies the periodicity and deadlines for the transmission of data to Fund staff for program monitoring purposes.

## PROGRAM ASSUMPTIONS

1. Program exchange rates. For the purposes of this TMU, the value of transactions denominated in foreign currencies will be converted into the national currency of Togo (Franc de la Communauté Financière Africaine (CFA) or CFAF) based on the exchange rates below at end -December 2023 (Table 1).

| Table 1. Togo: Exchange rates (end of period, 2023)  CFAF/$  607,04  CFAF/Euro  655,96  CFAF/SDR  804,80 |
| -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |

## DEFINITIONS

## Definition of Government and Public Entities

2. State. Unless otherwise specified, the term "government" is defined in this TMU as the central administration of the Togolese Republic and does not include local governments, social security funds, the Central Bank of West African States (BCEAO), nonfinancial public enterprises, and other public or government-owned entities that have autonomous legal status and are not included in the table detailing the financial operations of the state (TOFE).
3. Public entities. Unless otherwise indicated, the term "public entities" is defined in this TMU as majority government-owned companies, and other public entities receiving earmarked tax and quasi-tax revenues.

## Definition of Debt

4. Debt. The definition of debt, for program purposes, is set out in paragraph 8(a) of the Guidelines on Public Debt Conditionality in Fund Arrangements attached to IMF Executive Board

Decision No. 16919 -(20/103), adopted October 28, 2020 1 .

- a. For the purpose of these guidelines, the term "debt" will be understood to mean a current, i.e., not contingent, liability, created under a contractual arrangement through the provision of value in the form of assets (including currency) or services, and which requires the obligor to make one or more payments in the form of assets (including currency) or services, at some future point(s) in time; these payments will discharge the principal and/or interest liabilities incurred under the contract. Debts can take several forms; the primary ones being as follows:
- i. loans, i.e., advances of money to the obligor by the lender made on the basis of an undertaking that the obligor will repay the funds in the future (including deposits, bonds, debentures, commercial loans and buyers' credits) and temporary exchanges of assets that are equivalent to fully collateralized loans under which the obligor is required to repay the funds, and usually pay interest, by repurchasing the collateral from the buyer in the future (such as repurchase agreements and official swap arrangements);
- ii. suppliers' credits, i.e., contracts where the supplier permits the obligor to defer payments until sometime after the date on which the goods are delivered or services are provided; and
- b. leases, i.e., arrangements under which property is provided which the lessee has the right to use for one or more specified period(s) of time that are usually shorter than the total expected service life of the property, while the lessor retains the title to the property. For these guidelines, the debt is the present value (at the inception of the lease) of all lease payments expected to be made during the period of the agreement excluding those payments that cover the operation, repair, or maintenance of the property.
- c. Under the definition of debt set out above, arrears, penalties, and judicially awarded damages arising from the failure to make payment under a contractual obligation that constitutes debt are debt. Failure to make payment on an obligation that is not considered debt under this definition (e.g., payment on delivery) will not give rise to debt.

5. Public debt includes obligations of the central government and public entities. It excludes non-guaranteed debt of Port Autonome de Lomé.
6. Guaranteed debt. For program purposes, a 'guaranteed debt' is an explicit promise by the public sector to pay or service a third-party obligation (involving payments in cash or in kind) in the event of the debtor's default.
7. Contracted Debt. For program purposes, a debt is considered contracted when all conditions for its entry into effect have been met, including approval by the Minister of Finance whenever necessary. Contracting of credit lines (which can be drawn at any time and entered into

1 Reform of the Policy on Public Debt Limits in Programs Supported by the International Monetary Fund – 16919(20/103) (imf.org) .

effect) with no predetermined disbursement schedule or with multiple disbursements will also be considered contracting of debt.

8. Domestic debt is defined as debt contracted or serviced in CFAF except for borrowing from the West African Development Bank (BOAD), which is considered external debt even if such borrowing is denominated in CFAF.
9. External debt is defined as debt contracted or serviced in a currency other than the CFAF. Borrowing from BOAD is considered external debt despite being denominated in CFAF.
10. The present value (PV) of a debt at the time of its contracting is calculated by discounting the future stream of payments of debt service due on this debt. A single discount rate of 5 percent is used for this purpose as set forth in IMF Executive Board Decision No. 15248-(13/97). The grant element of a debt is the difference between its nominal value and its PV value, expressed as a percentage of the nominal value of the debt. A debt is considered concessional if, at the date of its conclusion, the ratio between its present value and nominal value is below 65 percent (equivalent to a grant element of at least 35 percent). For debts with a grant element equal or below zero, the PV will be set equal to the nominal value of the debt.
11. For debt carrying a variable interest rate in the form of a benchmark interest rates plus a fixed spread, the PV of the debt will be calculated using a program reference rate plus the fixed spread (in basis points) specified in the debt contract. The program reference rates and spreads are based on the "average projected rate" for the six-month USD Financial Institution Repo Facility (FIRF) as projected in the October 2025 World Economic Outlook for the ten-year period end -2025 to end -2035. The program reference rate for the six-months USD FIRF is presently 3.05 percent; the spread of the six-month Euro FIRF over six-month USD FIRF is -50 basis points; for interest rates on other currencies, the spread over six months USD FIRF is 50 basis points. These interest rates and spreads will be updated at end-2026. Where the variable rate is linked to a benchmark interest rate other than the six -month USD FIRF, a spread reflecting the difference between the benchmark rate and the six -month USD FIRF (rounded to the nearest 50 bps) will be added.

## QUANTITATIVE PERFORMANCE CRITERIA AND INDICATIVE TARGETS

12. The fifth and sixth reviews under the ECF arrangement will be based on quantitative performance criteria (PCs) for end-June 2026 and end-December 2026, respectively. The program establishes quantitative PCs for the following variables:

- Basic Primary Fiscal Balance (Floor); and
- Net Domestic Financing (Ceiling).

79

## 13. The program establishes continuous quantitative PCs for the following variables:

- Accumulation of Arrears on External Public Debt (Zero Ceiling);
- Government Guarantees on Bank Prefinancing for Public Investments and Domestic Loans to Suppliers and Contractors (Zero Ceiling); and
- PV of New External Debt Contracted or Guaranteed by the Government (Ceiling).

14. ITs are established for end -March, end-June, end-September, and end-December each year for the following variables:

Tax Revenue (Floor); and Social and Pro-Poor Spending (Floor).

## Performance Criterion on Basic Primary Fiscal Balance (Floor)

## Definition

15. The basic primary fiscal balance is defined as the difference between (i) the government's total fiscal revenue on a cash basis (tax and nontax) excluding grants and (ii) basic primary fiscal expenditure. Basic primary fiscal expenditure is defined as fiscal (current and capital) expenditure minus (a) interest payments on domestic and external debt; and (b) capital expenditure financed by external project grants or project loans. Spending on banking sector repair transactions of up to CFAF 27.0 billion or the approved amount by the Commission are excluded from expenditure in 2025. Expenditure is assessed on a payment order basis.
16. The balance will be calculated cumulatively from January 1 each year. The balances at end -June and end -December (performance criteria) and the balances at end-March and end September (indicative targets) must be equal to or greater than the amounts indicated in MEFP Table 1. The data are sourced from the TOFE, prepared monthly by the Directorate-General of the Treasury and Public Accounting of the Ministry of Economy and Finance.
17. Adjustor. The floor on the basic primary fiscal balance will be adjusted downwards by the amount of additional external budget support (grants and loans) that exceeds the projected external budget support for the program (MEFP Table 1), up to a maximum of CFAF 20 billion in 2026.

## Reporting Deadlines

18. Detailed data concerning the basic primary fiscal balance will be reported monthly within eight weeks of the end of the month .

## Performance Criterion on Net Domestic Financing (Ceiling)

## Definition

19. Government net domestic financing is defined as the sum of net banking sector credit to the government plus net domestic nonbank financing of the government. Net domestic financing will be calculated cumulatively from January 1 of each year. Net domestic financing at end-June and end -December (PC) and end-March and end-September (IT) must be equal to or less than the amounts indicated in Table 1 of the MEFP.

- Net credit from the banking sector to the government is equal to the change in the balance of government claims and debts to national banking institutions in Togo. Government claims include balances in the Togolese Treasury, Treasury deposits at the central bank, Treasury deposits in commercial banks (excluding the deposits of other arms of government such as deposits from projects financed with external resources and CNSS accounts), and blocked accounts. Government debts to the banking system include assistance from the central bank (excluding BCEAO credits to the government tied to IMF financing), assistance from commercial banks (including government securities denominated in CFA francs held by commercial banks calculated on the basis of first issuance excluding secondary market operations) and deposits in postal current accounts.
- Net domestic nonbank financing of the government is calculated in accordance with the statistical TOFE: (i) change in the stock of government securities issued in CFA francs (including on the WAEMU regional financial market) not held by Togolese investors, calculated on the basis of initial subscriptions; (ii) repayment of other domestic public debt (including banks' government -assumed claims on the economy and securitized arrears) to nonbank entities (including nonresidents); and privatization proceeds. The assumption or securitization of debts and arrears by the government is not included in the definition of net domestic financing, whereas the amortization of this debt by the government is included. To focus on net domestic financing resulting from transactions under the control of the central government and to avoid situations where transactions between the central government and other elements of the public sector pollute the domestic financing data, changes in the Treasury correspondents' deposit accounts, in the various Treasury deposit and consignment accounts, and in the accounts on which fines and convictions are deposited pending settlement are excluded.

20. Tolerance margin. The ceiling on net domestic financing of the government will be set at the programmed level of domestic financing plus a margin of CFAF 30 billion (0.5 percent of GDP). The objective of this margin is to enable appropriate below-the-line transactions, such as public institution spending based on budget allocations received in previous years, while avoiding excessive debt growth.
21. Adjustor. The ceiling on net domestic financing will be adjusted downward by the amount of the surplus of external budget support grants and concessional loans received over those provided for in the program (CFAF 83.4 billion) in 2026, minus CFAF 18 billion, and by the full amount of any non-concessional non-project loans ("other commercial loans") that exceed the program amount of CFAF [262.4] billion. This adjustment aims to limit the accumulation of total debt (domestic and external) while providing incentives for the government to seek concessional budget financing. The ceiling on net domestic financing will be adjusted upward by the difference between the amount of external budget support grants and concessional loans and "other commercial loans" received and the amount programmed [(CFAF 346 billion = CFAF 83.4 billion + CFAF 262.4 billion)], up to a maximum of CFAF 50 billion in 2026. The purpose of this adjustor is to allow the government to take up more domestic borrowing when there is a shortfall of programmed external borrowing for budget financing, without overtaxing the domestic market. IMF disbursements shall be excluded for the purpose of these adjustors.
22. Data are reported in the statistical TOFE prepared monthly by the General Directorate of the Treasury and Public Accounting of the Ministry of Economy and Finance. Net credit from the banking sector to the government is calculated by the TOFE unit, using the amount of treasury bills and bonds determined by UMOA-Titres at issuance. The government's net domestic non-bank financing will be calculated by the General Directorate of the Treasury and Public Accounting of the Ministry of Economy and Finance, which will ensure that all data on national borrowing are consistent between the TOFE and the data reported by the Directorate of Public Debt and Financing. Their data will be prioritized for program purposes. Data on domestic borrowing will be categorized as short term (less than one year) and medium and long term (one year or more).

## Reporting Deadlines

23. Data concerning net domestic financing of the government will be reported monthly within eight weeks of the end of the month. Similarly, data on domestic borrowing through issuance of negotiable debt and government bank borrowing will be reported monthly within eight weeks of the end of each month.

## Performance Criterion on Arrears on External Public Debt (Ceiling)

## Definition

24. The government will not accumulate payment arrears on external public debt (continuous PC). For the purpose of this PC, arrears are defined as external debt obligations of the government that have not been paid when due in accordance with the relevant contractual terms (taking into account any contractual grace periods). This PC excludes arrears on external financial obligations of the government subject to rescheduling or an agreed debt service suspension. This criterion excludes arrears on debts subject to dispute or renegotiation. The source of the data is the Public Debt and Financing Directorate. The government will report accumulation of any new external payment arrears immediately to Fund staff.

## Performance Criterion on Government Guarantees of Bank Prefinancing for Public Investments and Domestic Loans to Suppliers and Contractors (Zero Ceiling)

## Definition

25. The government undertakes not to guarantee any new bank pre-financing for public investments and not to provide any new financial guarantees for domestic loans to its suppliers or contractors (continuous PC). The concept of "government" used for this performance criterion includes the definition of government in paragraph 2, public institutions of an industrial or commercial nature (EPIC), public administrative agencies (EPA), public scientific and technical institutions, public professional establishments, public health agencies, local authorities, public enterprises, national corporations (public corporations with financial autonomy, in which the government holds at least 50 percent of the capital), and state agencies.
26. In a typical pre-financing arrangement, a private company granted a public works contract by the government obtains a loan from a domestic commercial bank or a group of commercial banks. The Ministry of Economy and Finance guarantees this loan and, at the same time, signs an unconditional and irrevocable substitution of debtor agreement to service all principal and interest, which are paid automatically from the Treasury account at the BCEAO.

## Performance Criterion on the PV of New External Debt Contracted or Guaranteed by the Government (Ceiling)

## Definition

27. A performance criterion (continuous ceiling) applies to the PV of new external debt contracted or guaranteed by the government. The ceiling applies to debt contracted or guaranteed for which value has not yet been received, including private debt for which official guarantees have been extended. Use of IMF resources and import-related trade debts ("trade credits") with a maturity of less than 365 days is excluded from the ceiling. External borrowing for debt management operations will not be subject to this ceiling provided that (i) the full terms must be communicated to IMF staff; and (ii) IMF staff analysis must confirm that the operations improve the PV of the overall debt (both internal and external) and do not worsen external debt indicators significantly before the borrowing is contracted.
28. For the purposes of this PC, "government" is understood to cover not only the definition given in paragraph 2, but also public institutions of an industrial or commercial nature (établissements publics à caractère industriel et commercial – EPIC) excluding Port Autonome de Lomé, public administrative agencies (établissements publics administratifs – EPA), public scientific and technical institutions, public professional establishments, public health agencies, local authorities, public enterprises, national corporations (public corporations with financial autonomy, in which the government holds at least 50 percent of the capital), and state agencies.
29. The PV of new external debt contracted or guaranteed by the Government in 2026 must not exceed the cumulative amounts set out in Table 1 of the MEFP. For information, Table 2 shows the authorities' external borrowing plan for 202 . Changes to this ceiling may be made (subject to approval by the IMF Executive Board) based on the results of the public debt sustainability analysis prepared jointly by the staff of the World Bank and the IMF.
30. Adjustors. Two adjusters will apply:

- a. Change in financing terms: The ceiling on the PV of new external debt contracted or guaranteed by government (cumulative from January 1 – December 31 each year) will be adjusted by up to 5 percent in case deviations from the performance criterion on the PV of new external debt are prompted by a change in the financing terms (interest, maturity, grace period, payment schedule, upfront commissions, management fees) of a debt or debts. The adjustor cannot be applied when deviations are prompted by an increase in the nominal amount of total debt contracted or guaranteed.
- b. Budget support and World Bank loans: The ceiling on the PV of new external debt contracted or guaranteed by the government (cumulative from January 1 – December 31 each year) will be adjusted:

i. Upward by the PV equivalent of the amount of additional concessional budget support loans beyond the programmed amount of CFAF 83.4 billion in 2026, and beyond the programmed amount of CFAF 55.8 billion (indicative) in 2027, provided by creditors other than the World Bank Group .

- ii. Upwards by the PV equivalent of the amount of any World Bank Group loans included in the borrowing plan up to a maximum of CFAF 270 billion in 2026, and a maximum of CFAF 150 billion in 2027 (indicative) . The ceiling may be adjusted upwards beyond these amounts to accommodate any World Bank Group loans not included in the borrowing plan (or loans that are larger than currently planned) upon the agreement of IMF staff, prior to the signature of the loan, that the loan would not worsen significantly debt sustainability indicators. For the avoidance of doubt, this adjustor does not apply to loans provided by other creditors that may be guaranteed by the World Bank Group.

31. The authorities will inform IMF staff of any new external borrowing in 2026 and 2027 that deviates from the borrowing plan.

## Reporting Deadlines

32. The government will report a summary table of all new debt contracted, whether subject to the limit or not, on a monthly basis, within eight weeks of the end of the month. The table should include the date on which each debt was contracted, all financial information needed to calculate the PV of each loan, and the government's estimate of the PV of each loan using the IMF PV tool. The government may consult with IMF staff at any time to confirm remaining space under the ceiling in line with commitments made in the MEFP. Where it does so, it commits to sharing details of all loans signed up to that point without delay, and details of loans being considered .
