## OTHER CONTINUOUS QUANTITATIVE PERFORMANCE CRITERIA

33. The standard continuous performance criteria are also binding and include the following prohibitions: (1) no imposing or tightening of restrictions on payments and transfers made for current international transactions; (2) the introduction or modification of multiple currency practices; (3) prohibition of entering into bilateral payments agreements that are inconsistent with Article VIII; and (4) prohibitions on the imposition or intensification of import restrictions for balance of payments reasons.

## INDICATIVE TARGETS

## Indicative Target on Tax Revenue (Floor)

## Definition

34. Tax revenue includes revenues collected by the tax administration (Commissariat des impôts) and customs (Commissariat des douanes et droits indirects). The data are reported in the statistical TOFE prepared monthly by the Directorate-General of the Treasury and Public Accounting of the Ministry of Economy and Finance. The revenue is reflected on a cash basis.
35. The revenue will be calculated cumulatively from the beginning of the calendar year. Tax revenue collections at end -March, end-June, end-September, and end-December 2026 must be equal to or greater than the amounts indicated in MEFP Table 1. The revenue floor is an IT for the entire duration of the program.

## Reporting Deadlines

36. This information will be reported monthly to the IMF within eight weeks of the end of the month.

## Indicative Target on Social and Pro-Poor Spending (Floor)

## Definition

37. Social and pro-poor spending as defined in this TMU includes expenditure executed from the Government budget (from both domestic and external resources), relating mainly to public interventions in the areas of education, health and nutrition, the establishment of social safety nets, access to electricity, water and sanitation, microfinance (small and medium enterprises), as well as law and justice, as laid out in the Table in Annex I.
38. This indicative target will be monitored through the table of quarterly expenditure provided by the Ministry of Economy and Finance.

86

## Reporting Deadlines

39. Social and pro-poor spending will be monitored on a payment order basis under the program. The indicative target for social and pro-poor spending will be calculated cumulatively from January 1, 2024.
40. This indicative target will be monitored through the table of quarterly expenditure provided by the Ministry of Finance and Budget.

## STRUCTURAL BENCHMARKS (TABLE 2 OF THE MEFP)

## A. Tax Policy

41. Prepare an analysis of VAT exemptions to determine which are progressive and which regressive (SB 4.1). The analysis should draw on relevant data on existing VAT exemptions and socioeconomic indicators, including household income and consumption patterns. It involves categorizing exemptions into essential and non-essential goods, followed by assessing how different income groups utilize these exemptions. By evaluating spending behavior, the analysis can identify which exemptions primarily benefit lower-income households, indicating a progressive effect, versus those that primarily aid higher-income groups, revealing regressive implications. The SB will be considered met when Fund staff receive a report meeting these requirements by the deadline. Although it is not mandatory, economic models may be used to simulate the impact of removing specific exemptions on government revenue and the distribution of the tax burden among various socioeconomic groups.
42. Abolish VAT exemptions generating annual revenue losses of at least 0.25 percent of GDP, preferably regressive ones (SB 8.1) . This will involve using the results of the analysis referred to in SB 4.1 to identify the exemptions to be eliminated that could generate at least 0.25 percent of GDP in additional tax revenue. The SB will be considered met once IMF staff receives the legal instruments used to remove such exemptions along with the factual evidence justifying the related expected increase in tax revenue (e.g., the analysis report on VAT exemptions), within the agreed timeframe.
43. Adopt a Medium-Term Revenue Mobilization Strategy (SB 8.3). This strategy should include: (i) a definition of the revenue mobilization effort over 5 to 10 years, in line with the ECF program, including the goal of strengthening social inclusion; (ii) an overall reform plan for the tax system, adapted to the country's situation and institutional capacity, including an overhaul of the tax policy framework to achieve the objectives set, (iii) a strategy for strengthening tax administration, including any reform of the OTR necessary for effective management of tax policy and promotion of tax compliance, and (iv) as well as strengthening of the legal framework. (For more information, the authorities are invited to consult the March 2024 Mission Note by the joint staff visiting FAD – AFRITAC WEST and the 2024 IMF report on the Tax Policy Diagnostic) The SB will be considered met once IMF staff receives a link to the ministerial decree from the Minister of Finance of Ministers adopting the a strategy that addresses these requirements within the given time frame. In preparing the strategy, it will be important for the authorities to continue to seek technical assistance from the IMF's Fiscal Affairs Department. To ensure the success of the strategy, it will be imperative for the country to commit to its steady and sustained implementation, backed by strong political will and sustained resources.
44. Based on the MTRS, adopt specific tax measures to mobilize at least 0.5 percent of GDP per year in 2027 and 2028 (SB 8.4). IMF staff will assess observance of this structural benchmark on the basis of documentary evidence demonstrating the effective adoption and implementation of tax measures consistent with the MTRS and yielding at least 0.5 percent of GDP per year in 2027. In particular, verification of tax policy measures will rely, inter alia, on the adoption of the 2027 budget law.

## B. Revenue Administration

45. Prepare a report on the taxation of civil servants that (i) explains why personal income tax appears low relative to ordinary personnel expenditures, (ii) assesses the compliance of withholding taxes with existing regulations based on annual salary declarations submitted to the revenue agency, and (iii) examines whether the PIT tax on civil servants could and should be raised and if so, presents a strategy for this (SB 4.2). The 2024 IMF technical assistance report on the tax policy diagnostic highlighted a significant gap between the civil service wage bill, amounting to CFAF 156 billion, and ordinary personnel expenditure amounting to CFAF 302 billion, as presented in the 2023 Budget Law. This difference may partly reflect bonuses or payments to civil servants that are not considered part of the wage bill and are not subject to the PIT. The objective of the report will be to explain this significant discrepancy, assess the compliance of withholding taxes with existing regulations based on annual salary declarations submitted to the revenue agency, and examine the timeliness and feasibility of expanding the tax base for civil servants' salaries, based in particular on benchmarking against regional and international best practices. The structural benchmark will be considered met once IMF staff receives a timely copy of this report containing the above items.
46. Reduce by half the number of operations (import, export and transit) subject to customs suspension procedures, expired and not discharged relative to the situation on January 1, 2025 (SB 8.2). Taking as an example, goods unloaded at the port of Lomé and subjected to transit to a landlocked country; the taxes normally applied are suspended under warranty. Failure of the shipping company to reach the exit border within the allotted time could lead to complications, including the presumption that the goods have been diverted to the domestic market without payment of taxes. The objective of the structural benchmark is to halve the number of expired and non-regularized operations subject to customs suspension procedures, by taking regular measures to control these non-compliances and apply the necessary regulatory actions. The structural benchmark will be considered met when IMF staff receives evidence that the stock of such operations as of January 1 st , 2025, has been halved within the time allowed.

## C. SOE Supervision

## 47. Adopt an order that requires the routine production of audited financial statements for seven key SOEs (PAL, CEET, TdE, UTB, LONATO, SPT, SNPT) within six months of the yearend. These financial statements must be attached to the fiscal risk statement document (SB

5.1). This SB refers to the Port of Lomé (PAL), the electricity company (CEET), the water company (TdE), the remaining public bank (UTB), the gambling company (LONATO), the postal services company (SPT), and the phosphate company (SNPT). The goal is to enhance parliamentary awareness of fiscal risks associated with these companies to promote better management and accountability. This should be achieved by adopting new legislation. The SB will be considered met when Fund staff receive a copy of the legislative instrument enacting this change by the deadline.

48. Finalize and publish a comprehensive institutional, financial, technical, and operational diagnostic of CEET (SB 11.1). In the context of the World Bank–supported project on organizational transformation and governance strengthening of CEET, this SB aims to enhance transparency within the public enterprise and ensure that the reforms needed for its financial viability are grounded in a thorough diagnostic. The SB will be considered met upon receipt by the IMF staff of the link to the report within the stipulated timeframe.

## D. Debt Management

49. Expand the coverage of the quarterly debt bulletin to include the debt of state-owned enterprises and government-guaranteed debt (SB 5.3). The objective is to raise awareness of SOE debt and government-guaranteed debt within the administration, Parliament, and the public, with a view to promoting transparency and, ultimately, improving debt management and accountability. The SB will be considered met when IMF staff receive a copy of the first report expanding the scope of the quarterly debt bulletin to include SOE debt and government-guaranteed debt within the specified timeframe.

## 50. Update the Medium-Term Debt Management Strategy (MTDS) to include the reasons

for deviations from previous targets and annual borrowing plans (SB 5.2). The updated MTDS should provide a comparison of external and domestic borrowing in 2024 by creditor category against outturns for 2023 and the optimal strategy set out in the MTDS for 2024–26. It should also include a comparison of actual domestic issuances with the 2024 domestic borrowing plan as well as external borrowing relative to the 2024 present value ceiling on external debt. In addition, the MTDS should discuss the reasons for significant deviations from the borrowing plan and outline the actions planned for 2025 to ensure that MTDS objectives are achieved. The SB will be considered met when IMF staff receive a copy of the updated MTDS that meets these criteria within the specified timeframe.

## E. Strengthening Public Financial Management

51. Prepare a report on the reasons for unexplained debt accumulation over 2017–24 and a strategy for ending it (SB 9.1). The report should provide an analysis of the reasons why public debt increased by more than expected over this period. It should include a quantified breakdown of contributory f actors each year, examining the evolution of the overall fiscal deficit, below-the-line transactions, and evolution of SOE debt, and identifying other factors. Once the factors have been fully accounted for and explained, the report should include a strategy for containing these factors, with actionable recommendations for the FY2026 budget. Furthermore, the report should include commitments to carry out the analysis at the end of each financial year and report to Parliament as part of the budgeting process. Early drafts of the report can be shared with IMF staff for consultation. The SB will be considered met when IMF staff received the report that meets these criteria by end-December 2025
52. For 2025: (i) publish, in the debt statistical bulletin, the stock-flow adjustment; and publish a report on government financial operations, including an explanation of the discrepancy between the PNT (TOFE) and the PNG (BCEAO) (SB 9.2). The objective of this exercise is to ensure consistency across these different data sources and to explain any discrepancies, following the example of the note published as a prior action for the completion of the second review that established coherence for fiscal year 2024. The report should also explain the reasons for differences between the net position of the banking sector vis a vis the central government, as reported in the TOFE (PNT), and the general government, as reported by the BCEAO (PNG) and include a disaggregation of the differences in terms of institutional coverage and any mis -classification by banks. Finally, the report should include a dedicated section that explains the reason for the sharp increase in bank credits over the period 2022-25 reported by the BCEAO (PNG) compared with the growth in bank credits in the PNT. SB 9.2 will be considered met if IMF staff receive the link to the government's website where this report is published no later than June 30, 2026.

## 53. Launch the pilot phase of the Social Registry of Individuals and Households (RSPM)

(SB 13.1). The objective of this SB is to ensure the effective implementation of social policies, by protecting the most vulnerable through reliable and comprehensive targeting. The pilot phase of the RSPM aims to ensure the robustness of the registry and support effective implementation. This phase may be supported by the World Bank. The SB will be considered met upon receipt of official communication from the competent authorities confirming the launch of the pilot phase.

## F. Governance and Business Environment

54. Amend the public procurement legal framework to require collection and publication of the names and nationalities of beneficial owners of companies awarded procurement contracts (SB 6.1). The objective is to start addressing risks identified in the 2022 GI B 's Mutual Evaluation Report. This will require amending the legal framework such that it provides for the collection and publication of beneficial ownership information of companies awarded public procurement contracts at a centralized registry/platform, and for ensuring the availability of this information to competent authorities in a timely manner, in line with the revised international FATF standards. The SB will be considered met when Fund staff receive confirmation of cabinet approval of the legislative instrument implementing this change and its submission to Parliament by the indicated deadline.
55. Publish the Governance Diagnostic Assessment Report (SB 10.1). This report will be prepared by IMF experts with the help of technical assistance already requested by the authorities. The SB will be considered met once IMF staff receives the link to the website of the government where the report has been published within the specified time frame.

## G. Reform of a Bank

56. In consultation with Fund staff, adopt a plan for the restructuring of the remaining public bank that ensures prudent and independent management aimed at ensuring the bank's profitability and stability (unless the bank has been sold by then) (SB 7.2). This SB is proposed for deletion.
57. Implement the plan for the restructuring of the remaining public bank (unless the bank has been sold by then) (SB 7.3). This SB is proposed for deletion.
58. Launch an independent Asset Quality Review (AQR) for a financial institution (prior action) . IMF staff will consider this PA to be met upon receipt of a signed contract between the authorities and an independent firm, in line with the terms of reference.
59. Complete an AQR for the financial institution (SB 12.1): IMF staff will consider this structural benchmark to be met upon receipt of the final report by an independent firm, consistent with the terms of reference.
60. Use the AQR findings to choose a credible strategy for the financial institution (SB 12.2). The SB will be considered met upon reception of a formal and official decision on the selected credible strategy based on the AQR findings.
61. Develop a detailed credible strategy for the financial institution (SB 12.3) . The SB will be considered met once IMF staff receive a copy of a detailed credible and implementable strategy .

## H. Information for Program Monitoring

62. The authorities will report to the IMF staff any information needed for effective monitoring of the implementation of economic policies

June 11, 2026

## TOGO

THIRD AND FOURTH REVIEWS UNDER THE ARRANGEMENT UNDER THE EXTENDED CREDIT FACILITY, REQUESTS FOR WAIVERS OF NONOBSERVANCE OF A QUANTITATIVE PERFORMANCE CRITERIA, AND MODIFICATION OF QUANTITATIVE PERFORMANCE CRITERIA—DEBT SUSTAINABILITY ANALYSIS

## Approved By

Vitaliy Kramarenko and Jacques Miniane (IMF), and Manuela Francisco and Seynabou Sakho (IDA)

Prepared by the staff of the International Monetary Fund and the International Development Association. 1

| ## Joint Bank-Fund Debt Sustainability Analysis  Risk of external debt distress  Moderate  Overall risk of debt distress  Moderate  Granularity in the risk rating  Limited space to absorb shocks  Application of judgment  Yes  –  mitigation of breaches |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |

This Debt Sustainability Analysis (DSA) assesses Togo to be at moderate risk of both external debt distress and overall public debt distress, unchanged from the last DSA. 2 Two breaches of the external debt service-to-revenue threshold in the baseline over 2026-27 signal a higher risk but judgement is applied to maintain an assessment of moderate risk. This is because the breaches reflect the rollover of a single loan on the same terms that does not involve cash outlays. All other DSA ratios remain below the indicative benchmark and thresholds under the baseline and stress tests. Debt is considered sustainable because (i) the present value (PV) of public debt to GDP remains below the benchmark, and all but one external DSA indicators remain below their respective thresholds under the baseline scenario and stress tests throughout the projection period; (ii) all DSA indicators trend downwards over the medium term; and (iii) Togo's access to the WAEMU market and support from regional debt institutions bolster the country's financing options and debt management capacity. The authorities should continue revenue mobilization efforts, s, reduce near -term liquidity risks, and build space to absorb shocks; step up efforts to contain non-deficit drivers of debt accumulation and reduce fiscal risks from underperforming SOEs; and pursue structural reforms to encourage more FDI and boost exports.

1 In collaboration with the Togolese authorities.

2 June 2025: IMF Country Report No. 2025/1785 . Togo is assessed as having a 'strong' debt carrying capacity, based on a Composite Indicator of 3.21 that uses the 2024 CPIA score and April 2026 WEO data .

## PUBLIC DEBT COVERAGE

## 1. Togo's public debt includes obligations of the central government and public entities .

This DSA includes obligations of the central government, including guaranteed debt, loans on-lent to state -owned enterprises (SOEs), and collateralized debt amounting to 0 . 9 percent of GDP at end2025 in relation to Lomé airport. SOE debt, estimated at 1 . 0 percent of GDP at end-2025, is included except the non-guaranteed debt of the Port of Lomé (PAL). 3 Local authorities cannot contract new debt , and the central bank of the currency union (BCEAO) does not issue debt on behalf of its member countries. A residual stock of domestic debt of liquidated public enterprises, equivalent to 0.5 percent of GDP, is included in the debt portfolio, which the authorities are addressing . 4 The DSA uses a currency -based definition of external and domestic debt, in common with other WAEMU members, because of data issues, except for debt (even in CFAF) owed to the West African Development Bank (BOAD), which is treated as external. 5

## 2. Contingent liability stress tests are applied to assess risks that are not included in the

public debt perimeter. Although SOE debt is included in the debt perimeter, the SOE debt stress test is included because of limited information about SOE financial performance and contingent fiscal risk related to the public pension fund (CRT), to which the state has social contribution arrears. The default value is reduced to 1 percent of GDP because projections of energy sector deficits are included in the baseline. The standard 35 percent shock is applied to the 19 percent of GDP publicprivate partnership (PPP) stock that is reported in the World Bank's PPP database, giving a PPP shock of 6.7 percent of GDP , notwithstanding the fact that half of the PPP stock relates to PAL, which constitutes limited contingent fiscal risk . The financial market stress test is increased to 8 percent of GDP to cover any additional needs that may materialize in the financial sector considering persistent weaknesses and recurrent needs in recent years . 6

3 The authorities have included SOE debt in their debt bulletin (a structural benchmark under the IMF ECF) since December 2024. PAL has been excluded from the DSA since 2024 because it has a low fiscal risk based on its financial strength, it has borrowed in some circumstances without a guarantee, and staff has not identified uncompensated quasi-fiscal activities (see Box 1 in IMF Country Report No. 24/299).

4 This DSA includes this stock pending a ruling on these legacy debts . Most of the historic arrears to domestic suppliers were removed from the debt portfolio in 2025 in accordance with regulations in force, as no claims had been forthcoming for more than four years.

5 BOAD debt was reclassified from domestic to external debt in 2020 for all countries in the WAEMU region. The authorities report that 42 percent of securities that it issued in 2025 were purchased by non-Togolese financial institutions resident in WAEMU at end -2025, down from 62 percent in 2024. The regional debt issuance organization, UMOA -Titres, reports that 77 percent of Togolese government securities were held by residents of other WAEMU countries at end -2025 (up from 60 percent in 2018), but it is not clear whether this figure includes all secondary market transactions. The figure also does not cover the totality of Togolese CFAF debt.

<!-- image -->

## BACKGROUND ON DEBT

## 3. Public debt fell as a share of GDP in 2025, ending a period of rises on the back of higher fiscal deficits resulting from compound shocks since 2020. The fiscal response to the

pandemic, rising security incidents, measures to contain the impact of high inflation and ensure food security, and banking sector repair resulted in an average fiscal deficit of 6.1 percent of GDP 7 during 2020 -24 . As a result, public debt surged by 11 . 3 percentage points of GDP between 2019 and 2024, from 54.9 percent of GDP to 66 . 2 percent of GDP, reversing the debt reduction under the previous IMF-supported ECF arrangement (Figure 1). During this time, Togo also deferred CFAF 20.9 billion of debt service through the Debt Service Suspension Initiative . A A strong start to fiscal consolidation under the IMF -supported ECF arrangement reduced the fiscal deficit to 3.2 percent of GDP in 2025, underpinning a reversal in the rising debt trajectory. Public debt is estimated to have fallen to 64.9 percent of GDP at end-2025.

<!-- image -->

4. Togo has relied heavily on the regional securities market to meet its financing needs and vulnerabilities increased since conditions tightened in 2023. The share of domestic debt increased from 55.6 percent of total debt in 2020 to 61.7 percent in 2023, as financing needs were met mostly through securities issued on the regional market, where BCEAO support enabled Togo to benefit from maturities of up to 15 years and lower yields. The PV of the 2021 SDR allocation that was on-lent from the BCEAO to the government also contributed to this higher share. 8 The end of

BCEAO support, and tightening of regional market conditions in 2023 -24 amidst higher inflation and tighter monetary policy, reduced maturities and raised interest costs.9 In 2024, the weighted average maturity of new issuance shortened to 1.5 years, from 7 years in 2022, and 71 percent of financing needs were met by Treasury bills, up from around 5 percent in 2021–22, raising refinancing risks and exposure to changing market sentiment. In addition, the weighted average yield jumped to 7.4 percent in 2024 from 5.6 percent in 2022. Regional market conditions eased in 2025: the average yield fell to 6.9 percent and average maturity lengthened to nearly two years.

<!-- image -->

5. The composition of public debt has evolved towards more external debt, with a greater variety of creditors. After accounting for the reclassified BOAD loans, external debt increased from an average of 28.3 percent of total public debt over the five years before the pandemic to 41.1 percent over 2020-24. This mainly reflected higher borrowing from multilateral sources, notably from the IMF and concessional World Bank loans, which made up more than a quarter of total public debt at end-2024 compared with less than a fifth in 2018 (Figure 2 and Text Table 2). There was also a notable shift away from borrowing from non-Paris Club creditors towards commercial lending and, to a lesser degree , Paris Club creditors. Recognizing their high exposure to the regional market, the authorities are rebalancing their portfolio towards longer-term external debt at lower interest rates as part of their medium-term debt management strategy (MTDS). To this

8 Following the WAEMU-wide agreement, the SDR allocation is treated as domestic debt. It was on-lent from the BCEAO to the government with a 20-year maturity (with possibility of rollover) at a fixed interest rate of 0.05 percent . Its PV is, therefore, used to reflect its concessional nature (see 2022 Guidance Note for Fund Staff on the Treatment and Use of SDR Allocations). 93 percent of the SDR allocation was used for budget support in 2022 .

9 The weighted-average yield on newly issued government debt across WAEMU rose from around 4 percent in 2022 to a peak of 8.3 percent in April 2024 before declining to 6.9 percent in 2025 .

4

end, they signed new external loan agreements of around US$2 billion in 2024-25, more than a third of which concessional , that will disburse over several years. 10

6. Debt service remains elevated , though rollover risks are moderating . Interest payments rose to 2 . 7 percent of GDP in 2024 from 2.3 percent in 2020, driven by the rising cost of domestic debt . Lower issuance in 2025, partly reflecting reduced borrowing needs, helped bring interest payments down to 2.6 percent of GDP in 2025. Nevertheless, greater reliance on Treasury bills in 2023 -25 and the maturing of a short-term external trade-related loan debt have increased rollover and refinancing risks, with securities worth 7.1 percent of GDP maturing in 2026 . The slight easing of regional market conditions in early 2026, new external loans, and World Bank support in refinancing the trade loan has reduced domestic financing needs and reduced risks. Other notable near-term external debt service obligations are due on loans to Société Générale , EXIM Bank of China , and BOAD. Annual payments of between SDR 28 million and 50 million are due to the IMF over 2026-30 , against previous ECF arrangements .

7. The authorities have made progress in understanding other factors that have pushed public debt higher and are implementing a strategy to explain stock-flow adjustments and contain unexpected debt increases . 11 Staff analysis has found that, on average, public debt rose by 0.4 percent of GDP more than explained by the primary deficit, automatic debt dynamics and other factors over 2017 -24 . In May 2026, the authorities completed a report as a structural benchmark under the IMF -supported ECF that reconciled fully changes in debt with fiscal accounts over that period. The report found that, apart from fiscal deficits, debt accumulation reflected cash/treasury operations, valuation and financing effects, and coverage or timing mismatches between fiscal accounts and debt statistics. The authorities are implementing a strategy to strengthen fiscal reporting and cash management and are now reconciling fiscal accounts and debt statistics in quarterly fiscal reports and explaining stock-flow adjustments in the public debt bulletin , with some of these reforms supported by the World Bank SDFP PPAs . 12 Further work is planned to advance reconciliation with monetary data .

## BACKGROUND ON MACRO FORECASTS

8. Macroeconomic projections have been updated since the last DSA to reflect the end2025 position and recent economic developments . The baseline scenario assumes the implementation of sound macroeconomic policies and structural reforms, notably the fiscal consolidation under the IMF -supported ECF arrangement. The main assumptions are summarized in Box 1.

## Box 1. Main Assumptions in the Macroeconomic Framework

Growth: The macro framework incorporates the authorities' latest final national accounts for 2020–23, implying a sizeable upward revision to nominal GDP levels and the subsequent nominal GDP path 1 . Highfrequency indicators and full year output estimates also point to a material upward revision for 2024 and 2025 growth (estimated at 6.3 percent in both years). On the supply side, the tertiary sector is a main driver, particularly logistics, transport and trade services, followed by the secondary sector, while the primary sector provides a more moderate contribution. Over the medium term, growth is expected to continue to be supported by robust activity in transport and logistics, port-related services, the industry sector, and agriculture, while ongoing public investment and productivity-enhancing structural reforms should help sustain long-term potential growth. GDP-based fiscal and debt ratios are mechanically lower, all else equal. Growth is expected to slow but remain solid at 5.0 percent in 2026, reflecting spillovers from the conflict in the Middle East, before recovering to 5.3 percent in 2027 and 5.5 percent thereafter as the drag from the conflict

11 The Selected Issue Paper of the 2023 WAEMU regional consultation (IMF Country Report 23/102) found higher residuals using an alternative approach that focused more on above-the-line drivers of debt and used different exchange rate assumptions to allow for regional comparisons.

12 Since 2021, World Bank SDFP reforms have supported the country in strengthening debt management and transparency (e.g., public debt reports, MTDS, investor tracking database, enhanced investor relations), improving fiscal sustainability through tax revenue mobilization (tax expenditure reporting, digitalization, audits), and reinforcing public investment management (updated PIM manual, integration of maintenance costs and climate resilience).

## Box 1. Main Assumptions in the Macroeconomic Framework (continued)

dissipates and higher public investment and productivity-enhancing structural reforms bear fruit. The baseline assumes that higher public investment is implemented gradually and efficiently, consistent with sustained fiscal consolidation and supported by stronger revenue mobilization and productivity-enhancing reforms.

Inflation: Headline inflation (year-on-year) fell further in 2025 than previously projected, to 0.4 percent, with core inflation (excluding energy and fresh products) also falling to 0.05 percent. This decline largely reflected lower domestic food prices, supported by favorable rainfall and strong agricultural production, and is therefore not expected to be permanent. Headline inflation is expected to spike to 2.8 percent in 2026, in response to higher energy and food prices linked to the conflict, before easing toward the BCEAO's 2 percent target. Inflation is expected to gradually ease over the medium term, reflecting prudent monetary policy at the regional level, and lower energy pressures.

Revenue and grants: After a strong start under the ECF arrangement, revenue collection in 2025 was below expectations, mainly because of shortfalls in tax collection. Grants were also lower than expected. Projections incorporate the authorities' commitments to increase tax revenue by at least 0.5 percentage points of GDP each year between 2026 and 2027 through a mix of policy and administrative reforms. Planned measures for 2026 include introducing new taxes on selected agricultural exports, gambling winnings, and digital services; and eliminating VAT exemptions with an estimated yield of 0.25 percent of GDP. The forthcoming Medium-Term Revenue Strategy will guide further reforms .

Primary balance: Despite revenue underperformance, the authorities succeeded in reducing the primary deficit from 4.1 percent of GDP in 2024 to 0.6 percent of GDP in 2025, through cuts in non-essential spending. The war in the Middle East is expected to weigh on public finances through higher energy prices and increased support to vulnerable groups to cushion the impact of shock. The overall fiscal deficit is expected to widen temporarily before converging to the 3 percent of GDP regional target in 2027 with the primary deficit projected to stabilize at around 1.0 percent of GDP in the long run. The DSA also projects financing needs from energy sector deficits as other debt-creating flows over 2026 -30, after which time, the utility is expected to be covering its costs.

Public investment: Infrastructure and security spending resulted in elevated public investment of around 10 percent of GDP over 2022 -24. While reductions in infrastructure spending subsequently underpinned a reduction of investment outlays to 6.5 percent in 2025, a modest recovery in 2026 followed by an expansion thereafter is expected. Domestic revenue mobilization and the containment of current spending are expected to support a rise in public investment to about 10.5 percent of GDP by 2031.

External: Goods exports are projected to stabilize at around 14.5 percent of GDP, as the moderation of key export prices (phosphates, cocoa , cotton) from their pandemic era highs is balanced by expanding goods exports from special economic zones and service exports reflecting increased port capacity. Uncertainty around global trade flows, including future access to the U.S. market despite the recent extension of AGOA, may weigh on goods exports. Fiscal restraint and greater domestic production are expected to contain import demand, but projections for 2026-27 have been revised up to reflect higher energy import prices following the Middle East war. Projections for the current account balance from 2026 onwards have been revised down as a result .

## 9. Togo's financing needs will be driven by debt service and are expected to be met increasingly by external borrowing in the long term, in line with Togo's financing strategy.

Debt service is projected to be the main driver (90 percent) of gross financing needs in the next six years. The remainder is explained by primary deficits and, to a lesser extent, energy sector needs . The ratio will remain broadly unchanged over 2032-36 .

## 10. External borrowing assumptions are based on disbursement projections and historical

patterns . Expected disbursements from the IMF ECF (6 percent of new external finance over 202631) and IDA 14 (48 percent) use their respective official terms under various facilities . This DSA also includes the one -year EUR 150 million trade finance facility guaranteed by MIGA in 2025 that was rolled over on a cashless basis in April 2026 15 and a EUR 250 million commercial bank loan signed in January 2026 that reduced regional borrowing requirements . Remaining external project financing is assumed to be met primarily by multilateral creditors (including BOAD) and commercial creditors, whose share has been increased since the last DSA based on updated weights, with smaller amounts provided by bilateral creditors on terms broadly in line with those realized over the last five years. The overall grant element of external borrowing is projected to increase over 2026–29 , reflecting the lower financing needs and IDA borrowing, before declining thereafter, consistent with the authorities' ambitions to gain greater market access that may result in a larger share of commercial debt .

14 Under IDA21, Togo is classified as an IDA-only, non-Small State country at moderate risk of external debt distress. This classification determines the financing terms Togo is eligible for under the new "composite" PBA terms introduced in IDA21 to simplify the menu of financing options. Accordingly, Togo's country allocations are provided on 31 -year credits with a 6-year grace period, a 0.75% SDR service charge, and no interest charge.

15 This facility had an embedded rollover option which the authorities triggered in April 2026. The terms of the loan are unchanged, and no amortization payment or disbursement was made as part of the rollover .

## 11. Domestic public debt assumptions reflect recent trends towards lower exposures.

Ratios of domestic debt to GDP and debt service to revenue remain significantly higher than the median for low -income countries (LICs), indicating elevated vulnerability (Figure 3). 16 Despite these elevated ratios, Togo has continued to meet its financing needs in the regional market, though at shorter maturities and higher yields than in earlier years . Higher levels of external financing and lower financing needs result in a declining reliance on regional market borrowing, with declining net issuance projected to bring ratios closer to the median, whilst remaining above it . Given Togo's relatively small financing requirement, the regional market is assessed to have sufficient capacity to absorb Togolese issuances. Domestic financing assumptions reflect issuances over 2021-25, including a greater share of Treasury bills (40 percent). Bond issuance is assumed to be mostly medium -term (30 percent for 1-3 years, 25 percent for 4-7 years) with a smaller share from longterm bonds (8+ years), reflecting 10-year bonds recently issued on the regional syndicated market. Yield assumptions have been updated, based on recent data, with higher yields in 2027 reflecting higher potential borrowing needs in that year, should contingent liabilities in the banking sector materialize .

16 These ratios would likely be much lower if a residency basis were used to classify domestic debt.

## 12. The realism tools indicate that the macroeconomic baseline is broadly in line with Togo's recent history and cross-country distributions, after accounting for recent shocks.

- Drivers of overall debt dynamics. Projections of overall public debt are similar to the previous DSA and remain substantially higher than in the DSA of 5 years ago (Figure 6, bottom left panel). Public debt is slightly higher over 2026-29, reflecting the higher primary deficit path and the inclusion of energy sector deficits. The forecast error on debt was larger than for the 75th percentile of LICs and contributed to previous debt accumulation. The largest contributor was the primary deficit (bottom right panel), which expanded unexpectedly as the authorities responded to multiple shocks over 2020-23, and in 2024 when some below the line outlays were later included in the deficit. Better monitoring of other debt creating flows is expected to allow lower primary deficits to pass through to debt reduction in the next five years, with smaller

unexplained residuals. Higher economic growth is expected to drive debt levels down, whilst real exchange rate depreciation is projected to have a slightly smaller impact than in the past (bottom middle panel). Real interest rates are projected to contribute more to debt creation, reflecting tighter regional financing conditions and higher external commercial debt.

- External debt. Projections are higher than the previous DSA over 2026-31 because this DSA assumes a higher share of financing needs being met with external borrowing. Projections remain significantly higher than in the DSA of 5 years ago. The forecast error is close to the LIC median, but the large residual was the main contributor to unexpected changes in debt (Figure 6 top right panel). This partly reflects the augmentation of the last tranche of the previous ECF, the reclassification of BOAD as an external creditor in 2020, and the trade loan and assumption of debt from a hotel that the authorities took ownership of in 2024. In terms of debt dynamics, the larger current account deficit is projected to create more financing needs than in the past, whilst growth is projected to be a moderately stronger factor in decreasing external debt than in the past, along with exchange rate dynamics (top middle panel).
- Fiscal adjustment. The underlying three-year adjustment in the primary balance over 2025–28 is -0.4 percent of GDP (Figure 7 top left panel)—a reversal since the previous DSA because of the large adjustment in 2025 and moderate loosening of the overall deficit to accommodate the current energy price shock in 2026. Clearly identified tax policy and administration measures described in Box 1 should ensure limited deviations from this path, though the realization of contingent liabilities in the banking sector could result in a temporarily higher deficit that would be financed through regional issuance. Continued security risks could result in higher-thanexpected security-related capital spending, whilst persistently higher energy prices, or secondround impacts on overall inflation, could also add fiscal strain beyond that in the baseline .
- Fiscal adjustment and growth . Growth is now projected to be lower than the projection under all fiscal multipliers in 2026 (Figure 7 top right panel) despite the slight fiscal loosening , reflecting possible disruption to global trade and partial pass-through of higher fuel prices in the second half of the year . The projection is considered to be credible based on high-frequency data in the first few months of the year (see Box 1) . It is projected to be in line with the projections under a fiscal multiplier of 0.4 in 2027 .
- Public investment and growth. Projections show public investment about 2 percent of GDP higher, on average, over 2026-31 compared to the previous DSA , reflecting the gradual recovery of capital spending supported by revenue growth. It will, therefore, contribute more to real GDP growth than in the previous DSA, though less than in the last 5 years. Private investment is projected to be about 1.5 percent of GDP lower on average (Figure 7, bottom right).

## COUNTRY CLASSIFICATION AND DETERMINATION OF SCENARIO STRESS TESTS

## 13. Togo's debt carrying capacity is assessed to be " strong " , unchanged from the previous

DSA . The composite indicator, which captures the impact of several factors through a weighted average of an institutional indicator (using the latest CPIA score, from 2024), real GDP growth, remittances, international reserves, and world growth (using the WEO of April 2026) was 3.21. This is higher than in the previous DSA, reflecting the GDP revisions, and remains above the cutoff that signals 'strong' debt carrying capacity. The external debt thresholds and total public debt benchmark are set out in Text Table 3 .

## 14. Standardized stress tests are run to determine Togo's debt sustainability rating. The six

standardized tests applied to both the public and external DSA using default shocks are: real GDP growth, the primary balance, exports, other financial inflows from current transfers and FDI, the exchange rate, and one with the shocks combined. In addition, the contingent liability stress test assumes a shock of 15.7 percent of GDP (see paragraph 2). The baseline debt projections and the projections under these standardized stress tests are assessed against the thresholds of strong debt carrying capacity. Togo does not presently show high reliance on commodity exports , high reliance on market financing, or other prominent economic features that would require additional tailored stress tests .

## 15. A customized scenario shows the impact of the continued realization of unexplained stock flow adjustments on overall public debt. For illustrative purposes, this DSA includes a customized scenario that shows the impact of recurring unexplained stock-flow adjustments on DSA indicators. This complements the standard primary balance stress test but is of a smaller magnitude.

-Under this scenario, the primary deficit is increased by 1 percent of GDP per year throughout the projection period as a proxy for unexplained debt creating flows. This additional spending is assumed to be financed entirely through borrowing on the regional market, 70 percent of which would be in the form of Treasury bills. Projections for all other macroeconomic variables remain the same as in the baseline.

## EXTERNAL DEBT SUSTAINABILITY ANALYSIS

16. All external debt indicators stay below their respective thresholds under the baseline except the external debt service-to-revenue ratio in 2026-27 . The two PV of PPG external debt indicators are projected to decline moderately over the projection period and stay well below their respective thresholds under the baseline and stress test scenarios. The PV of PPG external debt is projected to decrease from 24.3 percent of GDP in 2026 to 18 . 7 percent of GDP by 2031 (Tables 1, 3 and Figure 4). The downward revision to export projections means that the PV of PPG external debtto -exports ratio is slightly higher than in the last DSA but is projected to decline from 108 . 1 percent in 2026 to 82 . 6 percent by 2031 . Accounting for the rollover of the MIGA facility in 2026 results in elevated debt service over 2026 – 27. In percent of exports, the ratio remains below its threshold under the baseline and stress test scenarios. As a share of revenue and grants, the indicator is above its threshold over 2026 -27 in the baseline, partly because of the downward revision to revenue and grants. Both indicators improve thereafter, after full redemption of the trade facility . Triggering the rollover option again in 2027 would not result in a new breach in 2028. There remains limited space to absorb shocks (Figure 8), indicating the need to manage actively short-term liabilities.

## 17. The standard stress tests lead to breaches of the threshold for the external debt service to revenue indicator but all other indicators remain below their thresholds. All

indicators would rise over 2026 -27 under stress tests, with the PV of debt -to -exports ratio continue to rise until 2028, before declining thereafter , at levels higher than under the baseline. The external debt service -to -revenue ratio is projected to be above its threshold under the depreciation stress test over 2026 -27 before dropping below its threshold for the remainder of the period. It would remain above the threshold in 2028 under stress tests if the trade loan were rolled over again in 2027.

## 18. The results highlight the need to manage short-term liquidity pressures and increase

- revenue mobilization, whilst pursuing structural reforms to boost FDI and export growth. The depreciation. However, this risk is contained because around 70 percent of external debt is in euros

PV of debt -to -GDP and the debt service -to -revenue ratios are most susceptible to a one-time (including the MIGA facility) and CFAF (mainly from BOAD), and the CFAF is pegged to the euro. Nevertheless, strengthening revenue mobilization should remain a priority to expand space to absorb shocks. Export shocks would have the biggest impact on the PV of the debt-to-exports and debt service -to -exports ratios, highlighting the importance of efforts to increase and diversify exports.

## OVERALL RISK OF PUBLIC DEBT DISTRESS

19. The ratio of the PV of overall public debt to GDP is below its benchmark over the whole projection period in the baseline and stress test scenarios , mechanically signaling low risk of overall public debt distress . Despite the moderate loosening of the fiscal deficit in 2026 , public debt is projected on a declining path in the baseline, falling to 56.8 percent of GDP in 2031 from 64.9 percent of GDP in 2025. The PV of public debt-to-GDP is projected to remain below the high-risk benchmark of 70 percent of GDP, and the debt anchor of 55 percent of GDP under the IMF -support arrangement, over the whole projection period (Table 2 and Figure 5). Robust growth, fiscal restraint, and concessional finance assumed in this DSA would help reduce its level to 46 . 3 percent of GDP in 2031 .
20. Public debt is most vulnerable to the realization of contingent liabilities. The combined contingent liability stress test has the largest impact on all public debt ratios. Under that scenario, the PV of debt -to -GDP ratio would spike to close to the high-risk benchmark in 2027 (67 percent) and decline thereafter (Table 4). The PV of debt-to-revenue would follow a similar pattern. The debt service -to -revenue ratio would spike in 2028 and 2031, reflecting the maturing of Treasury bills and medium -term bonds that are assumed to meet the additional financing needs from the realization of contingent liabilities. Under the historical scenario, where higher primary deficits persist, all ratios remain higher than under the baseline. This highlights the importance of reducing fiscal risks, particularly by curbing banking sector weaknesses and SOE losses The customized scenario for public debt shows that unexplained accumulation of debt amounting to 1 percent of GDP per year would slow the decline in public debt despite fiscal consolidation and result in a significantly elevated debt service -to -revenue ratio that rises in the long term. This reiterates the importance of containing non-deficit drivers of debt accumulation identified by the authorities to avoid the need for further fiscal consolidation in the future .

## RISK RATING AND VULNERABILITIES

## 21. Togo remains assessed at moderate risk of external public debt distress and moderate risk of overall public debt distress, unchanged from the previous DSA:

- Three of the external debt indicators are projected to remain below their indicative thresholds under the baseline and stress test scenario. The external debt service -to -revenue ratio breaches the threshold under the baseline and standardized stress tests in 2026 -27 but returns below it thereafter . These multiple breaches signal a high risk of external debt distress. However, these breaches are driven by the triggering of a pre-agreed rollover of the short-term trade loan that does not involve cash outlays and are temporary in nature with mitigation in the form of the

embedded option to rollover the loan once more on a cashless basis. Analysis based on a single cash redemption in 2027 would result in a small, one-off breach of the threshold that would be deemed away. A single cash redemption in 2028 would result in a one-off breach of the threshold only under stress tests. On this basis, judgement is applied such that Togo remains at moderate risk of external debt distress .

- The PV of overall public debt-to-GDP ratio is projected to remain below the indicative benchmark over the whole projection horizon under the baseline scenario and stress-tests , providing a mechanical signal of a low risk of overall public debt distress. However , since the risk of overall public debt distress cannot be lower than the risk of external debt distress, Togo remains at moderate risk of overall public debt distress.

## 22. Togo's public debt is sustainable with limited space to absorb shocks to external debt.

Debt is assessed as sustainable because (i) the PV of public debt to GDP is projected to remain below the high-risk benchmark throughout the projection period under the baseline and shock scenarios, with a downward trend underpinned by fiscal restraint supported by the IMF ECFarrangement; (ii) all external DSA indicators trend downwards, three external DSA indicators remain below their respective thresholds in the baseline scenario and stress tests, and the authorities have pre -agreed mitigation to the breaches of the debt service-to-revenue threshold over 2026-27; and (iii) Togo's access to the WAEMU market and support from regional institutions bolster the country's financing options and debt management capacity . Under the granularity module used for countries rated at moderate risk of external debt distress, Togo is considered to have limited space to absorb shocks . This means that an occurrence of the median observed shock that affects external debt ratios could put Togo at high risk of external debt distress (Figure 8) .

## 23. The main risks to debt sustainability are fiscal pressures and the realization of

contingent liabilities. Continued progress in revenue mobilization remains essential to meeting spending needs within the regional deficit target and to maintain space to absorb shocks amidst heighted uncertainty . Continued security incidents, adverse shocks to growth and exports from a more uncertain global outlook, or persistently high global energy and prices from prolonged disruption to energy and fertilizer markets linked to the Middle East war could raise spending pressure and borrowing needs and trigger sub-optimal borrowing decisions that raise debt service costs and rollover risks. Stress tests indicate that Togo remains susceptible to the realization of contingent liabilities, particularly if weaknesses in the banking sector persist, whilst unexplained debt accumulation may necessitate fiscal correction . Finally, since external debt on a residency basis is likely to be higher than shown by data using a currency definition in this DSA, the risks around nonresident holdings of CFAF debt are somewhat understated. Judgement is not applied in this DSA, since CFAF debt is mainly held by WAEMU residents, which poses lower external financing risk than non-WAEMU holders, and this is reflected adequately in the moderate risk rating.

24. Staff recommends avoiding further short-term external debt until liquidity pressures decline, strengthening efforts to address fiscal and debt risks from underperforming SOEs, and tracking the holders of debt denominated in domestic currency. The authorities should continue to manage proactively short-term external debt service pressures; triggering another rollover of the MIGA facility would ease liquidity pressures absent other more favorable or longerterm options. There is a pressing need to resolve weaknesses in the banking sector, where there is a history of the need for fiscal support, the repeat of which could undermine the fiscal consolidation and debt reduction efforts to date. Similarly, the authorities should address losses in utilities, including by ensuring that electricity tariffs cover costs to preserve borrowing space. In the long run, improving fiscal accounting and adopting a public sector balance sheet approach would enhance projections of borrowing and reduce fiscal risks. Finally, in coordination with the BCEAO, the authorities should continue efforts to track the residency of the holders of regional securities beyond issuance and expand coverage to all regional borrowing .

## AUTHORITIES' VIEWS

25. The authorities agreed with the findings of a moderate risk of overall and external debt distress. The authorities recognized the elevated debt service over 2026-27 that reflects the recording of the rollover of the World Bank supported trade loan rather than cash transactions, and to avoid taking on further short-term external debt until a longer-term financing option is agreed . With the current MTDS running until 2026, the authorities agreed to update it to reflect the current economic environment. Furthermore, they committed to continue work to reduce contingent fiscal risks in SOEs and continuing implementing the strategy to avoid unexplained SFAs to lower the possibility of unexpected future rises in public debt.

- 1/ Coverage of debt: The central government plus social security, government-guaranteed debt, non-guaranteed SOE debt. Definition of external debt is Currency-based.
- 2/ The underlying PV of external debt-to-GDP ratio under the public DSA differs from the external DSA with the size of differences depending on exchange rates projections.
- 3/ Debt service is defined as the sum of interest and amortization of medium and long-term, and short-term debt.
- 4/ Gross financing need is defined as the primary deficit plus debt service plus the stock of short-term debt at the end of the last period and other debt creating/reducing flows.
- 5/ Defined as a primary deficit minus a change in the public debt-to-GDP ratio ((-): a primary surplus), which would stabilize the debt ratio only in the year in question.
- 6/ Historical averages are generally derived over the past 10 years, subject to data availability, whereas projections averages are over the first year of projection and the next 10 years.

## Statement by Mr. Ouattara Wautabouna, Executive Director for Togo and Mrs. Esso-Solim Boukpessi, Advisor to the Executive Director (OEDAW) on Togo

June 29, 2026

1. On behalf of the Togolese authorities, we express our appreciation to the Executive Board, Management, and staff for the Fund's continued engagement, through constructive policy dialogue, financial support, and capacity development . We also thank staff for the comprehensive report following the fruitful discussions held in Washington and Lomé on the combined third and fourth reviews under the Extended Credit Facility (ECF) arrangement. The authorities value the Fund's continued support under the ECF arrangement, which remains instrumental in supporting efforts to preserve macroeconomic stability, strengthen debt sustainability, and advance key structural reforms. Building on the 2020–2025 Government Roadmap, preparations are underway for a new medium-term development framework for 2026–2031, anchored in the long-term Vision 2040, aimed at accelerating structural transformation, doubling living standards, and reducing poverty. They broadly concur with staff's assessment and appreciate the relevance of the policy recommendations.
2. The Togolese authorities continue to advance their economic and financial reform agenda amid significant domestic and external headwinds and remain committed to staying the course.

Since the approval of the arrangement in March 2024, macroeconomic performance has remained broadly resilient, reflecting sustained reform efforts. These efforts have helped preserve stability despite heightened geopolitical tensions, including spillovers from the war in the Middle East affecting energy prices, trade routes, terms of trade, and financing conditions. Persistent security threats in the northern regions and increased vulnerability to climate-related shocks further add to this difficult environment. While the economy has held up well, these pressures are increasingly weighing on the outlook and constraining fiscal space, including through the 2026 energy shock, which is expected to carry an estimated fiscal cost of about 1 percent of GDP, reflecting higher fuel import costs and measures to mitigate price pass-through. Against this backdrop, the authorities are pursuing a carefully calibrated and targeted policy response to protect vulnerable households, safeguard macroeconomic stability, and preserve the credibility of the medium-term fiscal consolidation path.

## RECENT ECONOMIC DEVELOPMENTS

3. The economy continues to demonstrate macroeconomic stability and resilience, supported by the authorities' sound policies. Growth is estimated at around 6½ percent in 2025, broadly in line with the 2024 outturn. This was supported by robust activity in the services sector, particularly logistics , port, and airport operations, reflecting increased traffic and expanded handling and container capacity. Inflation eased markedly, declining to about 0.7 percent year-on-year by April 2026, driven mainly by lower food prices, though the delayed pass-through of rising international energy prices has yet to materialize fully. The financial sector continued to support activity, although vulnerabilities persist in parts of the banking system, with non-performing loans rising to about 13 percent of total credit, concentrated in a relatively limited number of banks. On the external side, Togo's position

remains broadly aligned with fundamentals. While export performance was weighed down by softer prices for key commodities, the current account deficit widened only slightly to about 2.7 percent of GDP, supported by lower goods imports. Regional foreign exchange reserves also rebounded to about 8.4 months of imports by April 2026, remaining well above adequacy thresholds.

4. On the fiscal front, policy remained anchored in the authorities' medium-term consolidation strategy and program objectives, despite weaker-than-expected revenue performance. Through well-targeted expenditure restraint, the overall deficit was reduced significantly to 3.2 percent of GDP in 2025, below the 3.9 percent envisaged in the revised budget and down from 6.8 percent in 2024, thereby supporting improved debt dynamics and helping to lower public debt from 66.2 percent of GDP in 2024 to about 64.9 percent of GDP by end-2025. Revenue shortfalls created temporary liquidity pressures, which were partly mitigated through refinancing operations, including support from development partners, and improved financing conditions. At the same time, the fiscal outlook is being shaped by the 2026 energy shock, which is expected to exert additional pressures, but the authorities' policy response should help contain the deficit at about 3.4 percent of GDP while preserving the medium-term consolidation path.

## PROGRAM PERFORMANCE

5. Program implementation under the ECF-supported arrangement has remained broadly satisfactory . However, following a 2025 on-site inspection by the WAEMU Banking Commission, undertaken at the authorities' request, the financial position of the remaining public bank was found to be significantly weaker than previously assessed. As a result, completion of the third review required additional time to recalibrate a credible strategy for the bank. The authorities used this period proactively to take decisive corrective measures, including appointing new management, establishing a private-sector-led supervisory board, and strengthening risk controls. They have also engaged in close and high-level consultations with IMF staff and Management to assess the implications and identify the most appropriate, well-calibrated, and carefully sequenced way forward. As a critical step in this process, the authorities have completed a prior action consisting of the launch of an independent Asset Quality Review (AQR) for the institution. This AQR will provide a robust basis for developing a credible strategy to address vulnerabilities, safeguard financial stability, and contain fiscal costs and risks.
6. Under the third and fourth reviews, most quantitative targets were met, and structural benchmark implementation remained strong. All QPCs were met under the third review except for the ceiling on net domestic financing, which was missed due to temporary cash-flow pressures arising from delays in external disbursements, including the ECF disbursement from the second review. Under the fourth review, all QPCs were met except for two minor deviations on the continuous performance criterion on new external borrowing at end-2025 and early 2026. The first was a technical breach related to an undisbursed end -year loan that did not affect the 2025 fiscal outturn. The second breach was technical in nature, reflecting evolving program parameters and financing conditions, while remaining consistent with maintaining debt sustainability and not affecting the program's overarching objectives. The authorities have since recalibrated their borrowing strategy, with a renewed emphasis on concessional financing and strengthened oversight of external borrowing.
7. The indicative target on tax revenue was missed, reflecting implementation challenges and increased tax expenditures, including the partial rollout of the 2025 revenue measures. Corrective measures have been undertaken, including tighter control of tax expenditures, strengthened tax administration, and progress in the preparation of a medium-term revenue strategy (MTRS). The temporary accumulation of external arrears in January 2026 reflected the non-receipt of a payment notice from an external creditor, which led to temporary administrative delays in processing the payment. This was fully cleared in March 2026, and measures have since been introduced to strengthen payment procedures and prevent the recurrence of arrears.
8. On the structural front, reform implementation has remained strong, reflecting the authorities' continued commitment to advancing key fiscal, public financial management, and governance reforms. Seven out of eight structural benchmarks (SBs) due since the second review have been completed. On the revenue front, the authorities prepared an analysis of VAT exemptions, assessed the taxation of civil servants, tightened tax expenditure controls, including through the rationalization of VAT exemptions, and reduced customs suspension procedures. In parallel, they enhanced financial reporting, governance, and transparency by expanding the coverage of stateowned-enterprises (SOEs) and government-guaranteed debt in debt reporting, strengthening public enterprise financial reporting, and publishing beneficial ownership information in public procurement. The remaining benchmark, related to the analysis of the drivers of previously unexplained debt accumulation over 2017–24 and the preparation of an operational strategy to prevent similar discrepancies going forward, was partially completed on time. While the underlying analytical report was prepared by the deadline, additional time was needed to finalize the operational strategy, which was completed in May 2026.
9. Considering these developments and the corrective actions taken, the authorities request waivers for the nonobservance of performance criteria on net domestic financing, new external debt, and the non-accumulation of external arrears. These deviations reflected temporary cash-flow pressures, administrative delays, and evolving financing conditions, while remaining consistent with the program's core objectives. The authorities also request modifications to the end-June 2026 QPCs to reflect the revised macroeconomic framework. Structural conditionality is being recalibrated in parallel to align with the new sequenced strategy for the public bank and to allow sufficient time for high-quality implementation of key reforms, including in revenue mobilization , fiscal reporting, and governance.

## OUTLOOK AND RISKS

10. The authorities broadly agree with staff's assessment while remaining cautiously optimistic about Togo's medium-term prospects. Growth is expected to moderate to about 5 percent in 2026, reflecting external headwinds, before gradually recovering to around 5½ percent over the medium term, supported by sustained fiscal consolidation,structural reforms, productivity-enhancing investment, and efforts to strengthen the business environment. Inflation is projected to rise temporarily to about 2.8 percent before easing toward the BCEAO target, while the current account deficit is expected to widen, mainly due to higher energy import costs and weaker terms of trade. Risks remain tilted to the downside given challenging and uncertain global and regional environment, and persistent domestic vulnerabilities, though a durable easing of geopolitical tensions could provide some upside. The authorities will continue to monitor conditions closely and stand ready to adjust policies as needed to

preserve stability and program objectives.

## POLICY PRIORITIES FOR 2026 AND THE MEDIUM-TERM

Fiscal Policy, Debt Sustainability, and Public Financial Management

11. Fiscal policy will remain centered on safeguarding macroeconomic stability, preserving debt sustainability, and protecting priority social and development spending. In the near term, the authorities are responding to the energy price shock, estimated to carry a fiscal cost of about 1 percent of GDP, through a balanced policy package combining gradual pump price adjustments calibrated in line with international price developments and expected to yield at least 0.3 percent of GDP by end-2026, non-priority expenditure rationalization amounting to about 0.5 percent of GDP, and targeted measures amounting to 0.2 percent of GDP to protect vulnerable groups. The pump price adjustments introduced on May 26, 2026, increased gasoline prices by 7 percent, diesel by 8 percent, and kerosene by 60 percent. These measures were formalized through a presidential budget decree, décret d'avance , adopted on June 8, 2026, and are expected to be reflected in a 2026 supplementary budget law. As a result, the deficit is expected to remain at about 3.4 percent of GDP in 2026, reflecting the recalibration of the fiscal anchor for 2026 to accommodate the temporary impact of the energy price shock, while preserving the medium-term consolidation path toward convergence to the WAEMU fiscal deficit norm of 3 percent of GDP. Alongside these fiscal efforts, the authorities are actively managing the social impact of higher prices, including through progress toward a unified social registry, improved targeted transfers, and a proactive communication strategy on the need for price adjustments and related mitigation measures.
12. Over the medium term, fiscal consolidation will be sustained to reduce public debt and create space for priority spending. To this end, the authorities are advancing domestic revenue mobilization, strengthening expenditure prioritization, and improving public financial management. The Medium-Term Revenue Mobilization Strategy (MTRS), expected by end-September 2026, will provide a key framework to broaden the tax base, streamline exemptions, strengthen compliance, and secure durable revenue gains of at least 0.5 percent of GDP per year. In 2026, revenues are projected to increase from 13.2 percent to 13.7 percent of GDP, supported by both tax policy and tax administration measures. Key measures include new taxes on selected agricultural exports, taxation of betting winnings and VAT on digital services, for which the legal framework has been defined. Over the medium term, the tax -to-GDP ratio is expected to reach about 15.7 percent by 2030. The authorities' mediumterm expenditure framework will be aligned with the revenue mobilization strategy, with current spending contained and public investment increasing while procurement digitalization will continue.
13. Priority social spending will continue to increase, notably under the emergency program for the Savanes region, combined with efforts to make spending more efficient. In this regard, the authorities are expanding key social protection programs, including cash transfers and economic empowerment initiatives, with a focus on youth, the elderly, and people with disabilities. Ongoing efforts to strengthen the unified social registry, monitoring, and evaluation systems, and targeting mechanisms will help support a timelier and more effective response to shocks.
14. The debt management strategy will continue to support fiscal consolidation and debt sustainability by reducing refinancing risks, improving the maturity profile of public debt, and containing borrowing costs. In this regard, priority will continue to be given to concessional financing where available, while non-concessional borrowing will be carefully assessed. The authorities are also advancing the regularization of legacy obligations, including historical domestic supplier arrears and debt related to liquidated SOEs. Implementation of the Medium-Term Debt Strategy is being further strengthened, alongside improvements in cash management, fiscal reporting, and debt-data reconciliation to enhance transparency.
15. On public financial management, the authorities are strengthening transparency, fiscal reporting, and the management of fiscal risks. They are deepening the analysis of past debt dynamics and improving oversight of SOEs and PPPs. These efforts are being supported by closer alignment with international standards, including the gradual adoption of GFSM 2014. In parallel, the authorities are advancing reforms in the electricity sector, a key source of fiscal risk and contingent liabilities. The Government has taken initial steps to improve the electricity company (CEET)'s financial position, including tariff adjustments in 2025, and the recent appointment of new leadership at the utility, as part of efforts to strengthen governance and operational oversight. With the support of the World Bank, a comprehensive diagnostic will be undertaken, and a sequenced reform strategy to restore the utility's viability is being developed. The strategy will provide a coherent framework to strengthen financial and operational performance, enhance governance, and address legacy imbalances. Over the medium term, it will support a gradual transition to a more costreflective tariff framework, improving cost recovery while helping contain fiscal risks and enhance service delivery.

## Monetary and Financial Sector Policies

16. The authorities are firmly committed to safeguarding financial stability and fostering prudent credit provision to support the real economy. Monetary and financial sector policies continue to be conducted within the WAEMU framework, with the BCEAO responsible for monetary and exchange rate policy. The regional framework supports price stability and external buffers, underpinned by sound fiscal and structural policies. While the banking system continues to support economic activity, asset quality vulnerabilities persist, with nonperforming loans still elevated and concentrated in a limited number of institutions. The authorities will continue their close engagement with the regional Banking Commission to strengthen prudential compliance, improve the operating environment, and enhance the availability of credit information in support of sound lending and financial sector resilience.
17. The authorities are pursuing a transparent and carefully sequenced approach to address vulnerabilities in the remaining public bank. Following the supervisory reassessment, they have recently commissioned an independent AQR to assess the institution's current financial position and inform a credible strategy. The AQR, expected to be completed by end-October 2026, will guide the strategy to address vulnerabilities while safeguarding financial stability, containing fiscal costs, and preserving confidence in the banking system. At the same time, initial corrective steps are being implemented to strengthen governance, risk management , and operations. Actions underway are strengthening governance and risk management by enhancing leadership and oversight. At

the same time, targeted balance sheet measures, including NPL transfers, reclassifications, and calibrated provisioning, are helping to restore financial soundness and contain risks.

## Other Structural Reforms

18. Governance, transparency, and business climate reforms remain central to the authorities' agenda. The authorities requested an IMF Governance Diagnostic Assessment, with missions conducted in August 2025 and January–February 2026 and are committed to publishing the report by end-February 2027. Its findings will inform a comprehensive strategy to further strengthen economic governance and anticorruption efforts. In the meantime, the authorities have continued to enhance transparency, including through beneficial ownership transparency in public procurement, publication of revenue administration information, and stronger AML/CFT implementation. The recently adopted AML/CFT law, aligned with the 2023 WAEMU Directive, will further strengthen compliance with FATF standards. Broader reforms to automate environmental permits, digitalize commercial dispute resolution, strengthen ta administration platforms, and modernize trade and port infrastructures will help reduce the cost of doing business and support formalization, while helping attract private investment and advancing Togo's ambition to strengthen its role as a regional trade and logistics hub.

## CONCLUSION

19. Our Togolese authorities have demonstrated strong ownership of the ECFsupported program in a challenging environment. Program implementation has been broadly satisfactory, supported by fiscal consolidation, ongoing reforms, and timely corrective actions. Looking ahead, they remain committed to preserving stability and strengthening debt sustainability while advancing inclusive and resilient growth. Given Togo's significant exposure to climate-related shocks, they look forward to continued engagement with the Fund, including on possible support under the Resilience and Sustainability Facility.
20. In view of their broadly satisfactory program performance, the corrective actions taken, and their continued commitment to the program's objectives , we would appreciate Executive Directors' support for the completion of the combined third and fourth reviews under the ECF arrangement, the requested waivers and modifications of performance criteria, and the associated decisions.
